McDOUCALL'S PATENT SELF RAISINC FICURISAR:AM. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 13, 1913.

Sir,—ln reading the speech of Mr. Joynson- Hicks , I was surprised to find a man of his position guilty of giving utterance to the follow- ing: "Above them is the Lord God of battles. In His Name and under His protection I say to you, the Prime Minister, with your arms, with your batteries—fire, fire if you dare fire, and be damned." No'wonder the "Manchester Guardian" referred to it as a "remarkable outburst." People of all shades of opinion have deplored the dragging of the King's name into party politics, and rightly so; hut when the name of the Almighty is introduced into poli- tical controversy it is nothing short of out- rageous. We English love a fighter, but he has to gain our respect, and such extravagant talk as I have quoted will only merit the contempt of the intelligent portion of the comMun,ity. ARTHUR T: HUGHES. 14, Bedford-street, Stockton Heath.

are supported by cadging—the blackest spot in our social system. How many are there who are unfit to be out of hospital and-cannot get- in, -because there is not room?

I sincerely hope that the leading men in the town will go into the question thoroughly, to try and bring about the reform whereby we working men will benefit by the knowledge that the hospitals are ours, supported by everyone of us. I may lay it down as .an unehallenge- able fact "that it is the prime duty of every man to protect himself against sickness," and by municipalisation of the hospitals he would do so.

PETER PEACOCK, juN. 44, Hood-lane, Sankey. December 10, 1913.

tary entry of the. Church in Wales into the jurisdiction of the Church in England, by in- clusion in the Province of Canterbury. Con- sequently the Church both in Wales and Eng- land is one Church, and named the Church of England, though it would be more strictly cor- rect, perhaps, if it were called "the Church of Wales and England," seeing that the Welsh portion of it is much the older. Assuming this to be trile 'history, how -does' Mr. Barker explain the inference which his first remark seems to convey, that the Church in Wales is not the " National " (Welsh) Church, but the English Church? And what does he mean when he follows on to say that it "spoke well for the nationalism of the Welsh that through the centuries they had kept their own peculiar methods of worship? And to further remark that many of the people in Wales "believed the Church of England reli- gion to be sinful " ? Your speaking contributor tells us that he is " theoretically " in favour of disestablishment, but he does not seem to have told his audience what he really meant thereby, nor, indeed, what he understands to be the thing which is called "Establishment." Respecting Disendowment, he is clear and frank—he does not oppose taking away 12s. in the X of the property, of the Church in Wales by Act of Parliament (seeing it cannot be taken away by the ordinary process at law) "because ,it would not affect the present parsons"! May ask him if he would have no objection to:con- siderably more than half his estate being un- deservedly ,taken away from him, if he were assured by his despoilers that the process should not hurt him during his mortal life? C. STIDSTON-BROADBENT, Diocesan Church Defence Organiser.

• • INCREASING RENTS. Sir,—Your correspondent "Trade Unionist? says that the speculative builders of Warring- ton have not paid the standard rate of wages to their workers •during the . last 15 years. I am a builder in Warrington and feel that this statement effects me just as much as it does Mr. .Smith. 1. feel it my duty to refute this absurd charge. I pay the standard rate of wages and always have done so. The same can be said of Mr. Daniel Cooper, my brother. Be- tween us-we have built as much house property as any other builder in Warrington. The con- ditions of labour on all our work are equal to, or better than, the conditions enforced by the union. When we employ men the question of union or non-union never enters into the ar- rangement, and the only., distinction between the two is that the bad worker gets his wages before the week-end. The bone of contention between the union and the speculator is the importation of foreign doors. The Canadian can be bought for 4s.- 9d., whilst the English costs Bs. 6d., or more. Why should the builder pay 88. when he can get equal value for -ss. If Free Trade is good for,the union joiner it should be good for the master, otherwise it is not equitable. Does "Unionist" suggest that none but union men can do good work? The timber in an inch and a quarter door (Pine) costs about 4s. 6d. Where does the labour come in? How is it done? An article on "Free Trade and Tariff Reform" might explain the whole matter. Anyhow, the word " Bunkum " throws no light upon the subject. In .answer to the statement made by "Unionist" that the speculative builder lives in a state of luxury is an absolute fabrication. Out of all the builders in .Warrington there is not one this Statement applies to, but at least half of th have met their creditors. This is a positive sign of poverty and not riches. Padgate-lane, Warrington.

WM. COOPER

. . . Sir,—The simplicity a your corresfiondent, "Fair Rent," is incredible. 116 asks, "Why should the .Warrington rents be raised to the Manchester standard, when it is admitted that the property here is valued at 30 per cent. less?" If he will carefully read my article he will see why. The value of property in Warrington is 30 per cent. below Manchester, not because it cost 30 per cent, less to Construct, but because the rents are ; considerably lower. A iihop in Bridge-street may not be as !arge or as well con strueted as 'fir 'cottage in Gorsey-lane, vet the rent may be four times that of the cottage. To arrive at the value of a property, we must deduct al.l the outgoings and see what is left. The second •argument used by "Fair Renter" is based on the portion of the cost of paving which should be borne by the tenant. He assumes• certain ratios to suit mnis method of analysis, and ultimately persuades himself- that sixpence- a week on a three-roomed cottage, pre- viously rented at five shillings per week, should compensate the landlord for interest on cost of paving and additional rates, which he admits should be paid for by the tenant. Before attempting to discuss this question, I advise the reader to keep in mind that the tenant 'pays the rates through the landlord; secondly, that the additional rent is subject to additional rating; lastly, that 5 per cent. is not an excessive rate of interest to charge for im- provements. If the paving cost £lB, then on this basis 18s. is due to the landlord. A cot- tage rented at five shillings a week is assessed at ..t.7 10s. "Fair Rent" admits that the rates have gone up is. 6d. in the .t, and seven_ and a half times one and sixpence equals ten and six- pence. and thus brings the total sum due for improvements and increased rates to 1 Bs. 6d. Take the previous rent paid by the tenant, £l3 per annum; deduct the. rates paid on behalf of the tenant by the owner to the Corporation, I'2 9s. This leaves £lO lls, is the net rent paid by the tenant. To this add Bs.. 6d. for im- provenients, and we have a total of £ll 19s. 6d. due to the landlord. The reader should ask the Corporation what the rates are. I have done this on his behalf, but there is'no reason why he should not do; this for himself,-if he doubts the truth of my figures. He will find that the rates are 024 125., which makes, the amount due to the landlord and the Corporation £l6 us. 6d., or, say, 6s. 4d. per week. The property owners sent a deputation to the rating authorities to discuss this outrageous anomaly in the rating of houses. It should be known to all tenants and landlords that in order to draw an additional rent of £1 Bs. 6d. per annum, the owner has, through the rating authorities, to charge his tenants £3'lls. 6d., and hand over... the difference to the Borough Treasurer. A "Fair Rent" has the audacity to offer his landlord sixpence a week, indulges in meta- phorical language, termil me an "innocent," de- clares " Nemesis " to be on my tkack. We are not nervous. " Nemesis " has been detected by the Association, and we have laid him by the heels. He is a "Fair Renter," who offers silt- pence a week instead of is. 4d. • There is not one reasonable argument in the whole of "Fair Rent's" letter. Epithets and names such as "Bunkum," "Rip Van Winkle," especially if made anonymously, are unprofit- able. Let "Fair Rent" come out of his shell, so that his lack of knowledge on this subject may be debited to the right account. I thank "Trade Unionist" for supporting me

in such a• valiant manner: Not that my ',argu- mentew.equired support; none of tnein nas even been assailed. if all , that " Unionist" says is true, I am Zetter oil than I thought, and the building trade is in a more deplorable state than ever. The cheapest labour, the cheapest materials, union labour and non-union labour, have tailed to extricate it from its deplorable condition, and to use " Unionist's ", expression, -it is a good job .it -has „gone .f o..the , I wonder whether the house he lives in was trade union built, and if it was, what addi- tional rent he pays on that account. The whole tenor of his argument is that the builder has gone to the wall because he did not employ union labour. He distinctly infers that the public will not buy house property because it is draughty and badly constructed, a condi- tion that would have been averted if union joiners had been employed. What vanity! Surely a new claifn on behalf of union labour. "Trade Unionist" would not, or could not, explain why the ,speculative builder, though paying the standard rate of\wages, was refused the support of the union. It is summed up in two words, " Canadian doors." The joiners' executive served notice on the builders to the 'elect that in future no union joiner would be allowed to fix Canadian doors. The co si of a Canadian door at that time was four shillings, whilst that of an English door was' eight shillings, and without the slightest doubt the Canadian door was the better value. I am not antagonistic to trade unionism, but I fail" to 'see how a man is made a better 'and 'a more skilful worker by joining the union: I can understand the technical schools 'improv- ing him, but I have yet to learn that Irade unionism stands for increased efficiency. It enables the indifferent worker to demand the same wages_ as his better co-worker, especially where day: work is the custom.

Much controversy has taken place on the ques- tion of the Canadian door. The union, either through sheer ignorance or stubbornness, re- fused to allow its members to fix or handle these doors. Most of the timber used in This country is imported. The cost of transit is considerable, and to facilitate handling and save space in shipping-',it iS cut up in -lengths and specified sizes. This cannot be done with- out leaving an enormous amount of small timber, which would never reach a market unless put up in a compact form. Not only does this practice 'save valuable timber from destruction, but it lessens the cost of boards and scantlings, an item of no small importance to the builder. It would be as reasonable to object to the importation- of flooring boards as doors, and it requires no genius to see that saw- dust- and shavings are not articles to import into this country., The speculative builder could not see the wis- dom of paying seven shillings for what could be had for four shillings. He refused to allow the union to dictate as to how he should spend his money, and demanded the same freedom in buying as that enjoyed by his union workmen. _ .

For the purposes of this argument I will desig- nate the man who has just received the price of his week's hire a capitalist. He belongs to the union, and receives something over two pounds sterling. He starts out to purchase his week's necessaries. Does he insist on the trousers he buys being union made? Does he ask if his brother worker 'received the standard rate of pay? Has the worker who Made his shirt, the watch in his pocket. the furniture in his house, the standard rate of pay? Does he care? Who patronises the cheap clothiers and the .bazaars, where all the goods sold are German? The man who refused to fix the foreign door did not hesitate to measure his lengths with a German rule, nor to saw and-plane his master's. timber with American tools. Had the employer who was compelled to pay him a standard wage insisted on his buying nothing but English goods, what would his reply have been? Surely he would have said, "I have Worked for my wages and shall spend them how and when I like," and who would have blamed..him? Not a Free Trader. He would'haVe resented such in- terference. That was the attitude taken up by the builder. . _ _

He refused to agree to the -terms insisteif upon by the union because the union did not live up to its Awn precepts. Can any reader believe that "Trade Unionist" is sincere when he-infers that if a union house was MAR for .him he Would pay any more rent on that account? My experience has taught me not to place any such reliance on my tenants. I hope to keep them by,tompetitton. •I do not trust a man because he has a loud voice and reiterates the one word "Union," but is a trade unionist only from Monday to Saturday, and between times a most uncompromising capital- ist. The trade unionist does not hesitate to wear a coat that was made under condition st a thousand times worse than "the cottage weavers ever dreamed of," yet he fastens the badie of his society on the most conspicuous part of the slop-made garment and sallies forth to teach the future aspirant of union oratory the fine art of "how not to do what you preach."

What is the remedy for this? Let every trade unionist buy nothing jaut union goods, stAmp all the goods with a union stamp, engage in- spectorS to check, as far as possible,- the goods bought and sold. In five years there will be few men out of the union and fewer masters still who would dare to be antagonistic to its prin- ciples.

answer to the question of increased wages for the industrial worker, and why I am in favour of the increase, the reply is simple. If " Trade Unionist" had invested in railway stock he would hope for a rise, or, at least, that it would remain at the price he paid. My capital is invested in labour (house property); if labour goes up the value of my stock will in- crease. Take the position- of the man who by Purchasing his own house has invested his capital in labour. If the cost of building is in- creased, the value of his house will go up accord- ingly. and if labour in all grades has advanced, he will have maintained his position and his earnings will be 'in ratio with the increased cost of living. The position of the man who has in- vested in Consols is bad. His money was ad- vanced at a fixed rate, and the invester Las no means of increasing the earning capacity of stock, with the inevitable result that the one- time " gilt-edged " security has declined in value, indeed, has fallen from ninety pounds to seventy-one pounds within the last few years. The obvious moral is to invest in labour by pur- chasing your own house. Socialists oughtmto feel happy at the result, for here We see 'the National Debt automatically extinguishing it- self pt the expense of the rich. Here we see the source from which increased taxes are drawn. In conclusion I must point out that it is very much against my liiclination to answer anony- mous writers. If a man is proud of his cause he need not be afraid of signing his letters, and it reflects no Credit on trade unionism when the armour of the crab is adopted to save the skin of its supporter. He accuses the speculative builder of never having paid standard wages, and distinctly infers that union men were never engaged. If he will prove that .his assertion is true, I will pay twenty pounds to the Infirmary; and if he does not lift this sum withifi a month, in spite of his shell, we shall suspect he is only "Larkin."

A VOICE FROM THE STATES.

Su—l shall be extremely obliged if you will allow me through the medium of your valuable paper, which I read weekly with inter- est, to thank the large number of friends for their good wishes extended to me.

I have read your article on Labour in your issue of November Ist, and I think that great credit is due to Mr. Clynes and good old Charles Dukes in trying to elevate the general labourer to a better position in the fight for a living wage. Now with two • such leaders it requires men to use the influence they possess to make the G.W. and G.L.U. army larger by inducing their fellow men to follow their example and uphold the "buttons up " principle. I should say that praise is due to a friend of mine in the good old town for the energy he dis- played during a labour struggle a short while ago. Has untiring efforts to bring forth a better issue was deserving of a better fate. "Nil des- perandum."

In regard to the brewery workers, only one word have I to say, " I have read with interest " A.S.'s" notes on Angling, and, as an old friend, may- I congratu- late him on the excellent way he contributes to "The Examiner."

In conclusion I 'wish your paper every success. P. TULLY.

Peoria, Illinois, U.S.A. must tell you that I pass your paper along to several men from Lancashire.

THE IRISH QUESTION.

you kindly allow me to assure "Phil Squod " I still adhere to every word of my expressions of opinion respecting his letters, as in my rejoinder to his first letter on the Home Rule question? And, again, it was the furthest thing from my thoughts to charge him person- ally with Pharisaism. It was just that senti- ment he uttered, comparing the wealth and in- telligence of Ulster, as against Home Rule, to the poverty and ignorance of the rest of Ire- 'land, who were in favour, that drew the remark from me in condemnation of that line of argu- ment which I have heard for so many years, ad nauseum, that my very soul revolts against it, because I believe it to be so inaccurate. As a matter of fact, four probably of the wealthiest men in Ulster are in favour of Home Rule and hundreds'of its business men. Ignorance is cpm- parative, and politically it cannot be laid to the charge of the Irish fieople. Their consistency for so many years belies that statement. Of course, as our friend "Phil Squod" rightly says, the 'result of this correspondence must rest on the judgment of your readers, who may have followed it. Therefore I want to reiterate, if possible, my opinion. The true aim of Home Rulers to-day is an Irish Parliament to deal with Irish affair', only, subordinate to the Im- peHal Parliament, and securing civil and re- ligious liberty to all within its borders.

PATRICK O'BRIEN 125, Wilderspool-road, Warrington.

MUNICIPALISATION OF HOSPITALS.

Sir,—This is a question of great importance, which, to my mind, is dpserving of every con- sideration from the ratepayers, and a question which will eventually lead to the greatest social reform this country has ever known. The question not only deals with "munici- palisation," but th 4 reform of our wretched nursing system. Hospitals and nurses are an absolute neces- sity to the publiB, equally as necessary as our schoolmasters, police, army, navy, but how are .these latter supported? Are we forced tip make special collections in the churches and from the various workpeople? No, they are "State- owned."

Vhy not municipalise the hospitals, and do away with the scandalous cadging methods by which our hospitals are forced to obtain funds? Let us municipalise the. hospitals, better the conditions of the nurses, pay them something approaching a living wage, do away with the ridiculous "living-in" system, get enough nurses to abolish it, and you will have plenty of applications if the conditions were altered and a"decent wage offered. What would the nurse be told should she ask for an increase in salary at many institutions? The answer would be, "Sorry, funds are low; have not sufficient to pay expenses." People do not realise that the nurse Performs noble work, the noblest in the world, tending the sick, and many times making an invalid of herself. Yet, she is the worst paid professional in the world. Why? Because our institutions

TRADE UNIONISTS AND TORYISM.

Sir,—lt is really interesting, and at the same time amusing, to read the speech of the gentle- man who toasted every section of the Conserva- tive Association at the annual dinner last Fri- day. The gentleman who proposed "Our Member" had doubtless read the fictitious menu that some characteristic Tory scribe had written for the occasion, when he said "he won- dered whether people were getting more ignor- ant, more susceptible to thestimulation of their passions and the loss of their senses." It is not a stretch of imagination to think that such thoughts were prompted by the tabulated abuse of the silliest kind against politicar opponents that was put in front of the leaders of the party in the shape of a menu.' But the most signifi- .cant remark fell from the gentleman who had to propose an omnibifs• toast' whh embraced every- Tory organisation in the borough. It was obviously thought advisable not to pledge thelm separately, or the consequences might he serious, and the speech even worse. •This gentleman remarked that bad organisation might lose an election. He had possibly in his mind the once famous Unionist Labour party of blessed memory, and its famous offspring, the Trades .Unionist Tariff Reform' League. It, is not,. to say the least, consistent with the optim- ism, and the professions of honesty of purpose of the speeches, to have a League without mem- bers, and a 'gentleman to answer on behalf of the League who is not a trade unionist. , TRADE UNIONIST.

NATIONAL SERVICE AND CONSCIENCE.

Sir,—Mr. 11. Gray writes in your issue of December 6th kindly asking me questions about conscientious objectors to military training in the Antipodes. . I gladly reply and give below the exact clauses on the subject from the Australian Defence Act.

Section 127, Clause (c) : All persons liable to be trained under sections (3) (c) and (d) of Sec- tion 125 of this Act, who are forbidden by the doctrines of their religion to bear arms, shall, as far as possible, be allotted to non-combatant duties.

Under Section 61, Clause (1), it is also stated that persons who satisfy the prescribed authorities that their-conscientious beliefs do not allow them to bear arms are exempt from service in time of war.

Mr. Gray must beware of the partisan state- ments with which the fighting seotion of the Peace party are flooding this country. He speaks gaily of hundreds of lads being gaoled. If he will come down to facts he will find that in New Zealand only 78 Territorials have been committed to detention under the Defence Act, and that in Australia those who shirk drill are one-sixth of the number of those who shirk com- pulsory education. As for his delightful idea that we in -Warrington, who were- largely re- sponsible for Lord Roberts's Manchester meet- ing, actually cabled the chief speech out free of charge to Colonial papers, Mr. Gray is too credulous. His suggestion is absolutely untrue. If Mr. Gray can bring any evidence to prove it, I will.give him a turkey for Christmas, just to show goodwill towards one of the fairest of our Opponents.

B. S. TOWNROE

Mere House. Newton-le-Willows. December 10th, 1913

===== 0002601_19131213_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
DISESTABLISHMENT AND DISENDOW-

MENT.

Sir,—lt was more than I Could do to under- stand the speech of Mr. IL Barker, reported in your last issue. He calls himself a I.,hurchman "High Churchman," to be correct—as a pre- liminary •to 'siding up (evidently for party purposes) with Nonconformist gentlemen who are desirous of depriving the Church of both position' and possessions! Would his political associates dream of being so considerate if the deeds of establishment, and .the endowments, of Nonconformist Churches were assailed?

Mr. Barker's first remark, as reported, was

that "the Welsh Established Church (sic) was not in any way the National Church, but merely a btanch of the Church of England in Wales." Apparently there is such confusion here that the only way to clear what may easily be taken to convey a most Untrue statement, is to state some actual facts. The Church in Wales is the • same Church which existed in ancient Britain during the time of the . Roman occupation. With the Ancient Britons, or Celts, it was driven into Wales by the next corners to the Romans—the Angles and Saxons. There, in Wales, the Celts and the Celtic Church have been ever since. Some people like to give their 'reputation away by calling that Church "alien." The Angles and Saxons, who were idolaters when they came to Britain, in their turn, be- cattle converted to Christianity by Augustine, who settled in the .country in, the year 597, as Mr. Barker correctly says.- In due course the Church of the Angles was embraced by all the kingdoms of the Heptarchy. And the "Church of the Angles" is the present "Church of Eng- land."

The only "establishment" either by the Celtic people in Wales of their Church, or by .the Angle-Saxon people in England (or Angleland, as it then was) of their Church, v.-as the accept- ance by both peoples of the one and only reli- gion or "Church" then known. It was long after the Church. was definitely formed that any complete constitution of the State existed. Nothing has • happened since completely to alter the respective relation or position of the two branches of the Church, beyond the volun-

G. SMITH

The Limes, Orford. December 11th, 1913

ECONOMY IN DRESS.

No man, unless he is enormously wealthy or terribly poor, can afford to neglect his appear- ance and dress. The first point to consider above everything else; is quality. In the matter of hats, boots and raincoats, a man who seeks real value for money could not do better than go to Jacksons'. In the case of a hat, it will retain its original stylish shape and smartness long after one of an inferior make is worn out; the slime thing, in a marked degree, is the case with Jacksons' raincoats and boots. At Jack- sons' branch shop, 22, Horsemarket-street, War- rington, a department is opened for ladies, who may obtain raincoats and boots of the latest fashion at the same prices as charged for men. It is not surprising that Jacksons' ladies' de- partment is being more appreciated every clay.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
SEQUEL TO THE "BLACK DEATH."

The most interesting paper delivered in con- nection with the Adult Schools this week was that given to the Sankey Bridges Women's School on Tuesday evening. It was another of the series on Old English history-, the subject being "The Black Death and the Peasants' Revolt." The lecturer said that it was a re- markable thing that so much attention was given by historians to the various Continental wars of England, whilst an event like the Black Death, which involved not' only the deaths of one-third of the population, but led to a revo- lution in agricultural and all industrial life in England, was passed over in a few casual para- graphs. The Black Death was first heard of in the East, and travelled westward over Asia Minor, Syria and Egypt, till it reached our shores, via Dorset, in August, 1348. By 1349 it had over-run the whole of the kingdom. Although' the figures' for the towns are very high, in London 200 per day was the mortality, and the coßntry districts suffered. most. Death overcame the victims in one to three days. Men and women were attacked, rarely the children.

A Chaotic Condition. The country was in a chaotic condition owing to the lack of labourers to till the soil, crops rotting in the ground. The few labourers left began to wander the country, and refused to stay at work. To .understand the consequences of the plague it must be borne in mind that for some time the manorial system, under which the majority of men were bound to the soil, was breaking down. They were beginning .to de- mand their .freedom and payment for their labour. Towns were rapidly rising up and down the country,. trade was becoming established; and men were specialising in crafts. The lamt- owners were in a fix, and they appealed to the king. Parliament. not being in session, he issued a proclamation -To the effect that all men must return to work at the old rates. This was after- wards embodied in the famous Statute of Labourers.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
A Severe Penalty.

The penalty of refusal was branding, and the landowner who offered to pay more than the 2d. a day enacted was also liable to-heavy punish- ment. The law was evaded by both parties. The country was in a state of unrest, and men were gradually breaking into rebellion. The spirit of revolt .was. fanned, into flame by such men as John Ball, the "mad priest of Kent," and Wat Tyler, and when a secies of poll taxes were instituted in 1379, it proved the la'st straw, and an active rebellion burst out. The riots were eventually crushed, but the laws relating to the holding of land were slowly changed, and there arose what may be described as a wage- earning class for the first time in this country. Next week the subject is "The Rise of the Towng."

The Bible lesson on Nunaay was on "Paul's boldness and confidence." It was pointed out that it was Paul's love for the Corinthians Which led him to be so severe in denouncing the vices .which'had-taken -hold of the Corinthian Church. Although Paul was a much Changed man since his acceptance of Christianity, yet his fighting qualities still remained. But now his aggres- sion was turned to other things. No longer did he use the old weapons. His defence of his allostleship formed one of the most stirring episodes in .his remarkable, career. Paul was still anxious to evolve the spirit of true fellow- ship among them, in spite of all the opposition he encountered. Although men might differ in matters such as church government and organi- sation, and on polity, yet love and service to- wards each other should characterise their rela- tions. Unity of spirit and purpose should_ever underly Christian activity. And Paul wat3 con- fident that in course of time these greater things would prevail. It was claimed that Adult School men should be broad-minded, charitable, and he ever ready to co-operate with other Christian organisations; working with them for the eommon good.

WINTER'S WORK ON FARM.

Most farmers find it difficult to keep their men occupied during the winter months,, and I know they would welcome any suggestions as to useful and profitable. work. Of course, there is always a fair .amount ,of work to do. Ditches and fences must be attended to, stock take up a lot of time also, and ploughing must be done, but op some days, especially when it is wet or snowy, it is hard to find work for all. This note may help to solve the problem.. If a farmer or his man has any taste for car- pentry, he will find plenty to do. Various ar- ticles can be made which require very little skill, and. not very large outlay, except for the wood. Feeding troughs and (O\V cribs are always useful, while farmers who keep poultry can construct chicken coops and runs also with wire netting attached on top. Although home- made appliances may not look very handsome, they have one great meril, they .can be made very strong, and they are much more useful than same of the .flimsy ready-made articles which one sometimes comes across.

The tarring, of woodwork provides- another opportunity of employing spare time. It is not much, but it certainly preserves the wood, be- sidts making wooden buildings watertight, and, moreover, it generally gives the farmyard a neat appearance. Fowl houses and chicken houses are similar. Buildings should certainly be tarred occasionally to keep out the wet. The tar can be bought cheaply in barrels, and it must be applied hot. The care of implements igain will furnish plcinty of work for the wintry days. Too often one sees farm implements neglected, especia4 those used for haymaking and .harvesting-; binders, mowing machines,

- swath turners, and so forth, very often when done with for the season are pushed away into a corner of the barn or shed, and left there till the next year, without any attention. The iron rusts, the, woodwork rots, the nuts get loose, and, in consequence, the machine. does not last half as long as it would do if a little care was taken of it. A little thought would enable many farmers to take reasonable care of their machines, and to occupy their spare time both pleasantly and profitably.

Is Seeing Relieving?

The Christian Sdentists and most Buddhists deny. 'any physical-existence whatever, and their position appears to them perfectly logical from a metaphysical standpoint. "The argu- ments of the Buddhists regarding the unreality of the Objects of perception," says a writer in the journal of the American Oriental Society. "may thus be summarised. Our perceptions in dreams do not, in principle, differ from, those in the waking state, • and consequently the lattermust be just as void and as independent of something existing beside them (their object, as the dream of impressions: further examples of impressions void of really existing objects are fata morgana and mirage. As dreams- or magic fata morgana•are regarded as unreal by ordinary men, so this whole world is regardtd by those versed in the Vedantas."

IN THE COURTS.

BOROUGH POLICE COURT

SATURDAY.

Before Mr. R. T. Fairclough (in the chair), Alderman Tinnion, Mr. T. Pemberton, and Mr. A. J. Jolley.

A CYCLE THEFT.—Fred Robert Kirk, 23, Ward- street, New Tupton, Derbyshire, was charged with stealing a bicycle, the property of Mr. Hughes, draper, 10, Crosfield-street.—The Chief Constable said Mr. Hughes left his bicycle in the garden of his house, and it was stolen. The police ascertained that prisoner had sold the machine for 15s. in Derbyshire. When charged, prisoner replied: "I plead guilty. I am sorry I did it."—Prisoner said he stole the bicycle to help his wife, who was ill." A letter appealing for leniency was read from the wife.—Prisoner was sent to prison for one month, with hard labour.

A LENIENT EMPLOYER.'-.Thomas Gahgan, 13, Gee-street, was charged with stealing 301 b. of scrap lead, value 35., the property of Leonard Aukland.—lt appeared that Gahgan was em- ployed by the prosecutor and had sold some of the metal. Mr. Aukland was not anxious to press the matter and was willing to take Gahgan back into his service if the Bench dealt leniently with him.—A fine of 10s., including costs, was imposed.

ITALY IN TRIPOLI.

Mr. W. K. McClure's "Italy in North Africa" is made up in part of his writings"as special correspondent of the "Times" during the occu- pation. The author is a defender of Italian policy and military action. He is able to show by his excursion into diploniacy that Italy's " claims " to the reversion of the Tripolitaine had the informal permission of the powers. What he does not clear up, and what may never be cleared up unless the secrets of the Chancel- leries are revealed, is why Italy took precipi- tate action in the autumn of 1911. He repeats the story, without expressing belief, or disbelief that Germany had sounded France with an offer to acquiesce in a protectorate over Morocco if France would give Germany a free hand in Tripolitania and Cyrenaiea, and that France gave Italy intimation of the German plan. It has been officially denied, but it holds the field in Italy as an explanation of the pre- cipitate seizure. _ _

-The fact is that Italy was cletermined_tograb

the country, and grabbed it then lest she

might find herself forestalled. Equity and fair dealing does not enter into such matters be- tween nations. I\l'r. McClure's narrative of the occupation, the so-called " revolt " of the Arabs, its merciless suppression, and such fighting as there was between the Italian troops and the Turkish regular forces, is chiefly interesting because of the strong light in which it sets the Italian soldier, of whom the author formed extremely• favourable impressions. 'Taking the campaign as a whole, it was unimpressive. The Italians played for safety. A peace was patched 'up without anything decisive having been accomplished. Turkey recognised the fact of the occupation of the coast, anU turned to meet her enemies in Europe. Whether the coast re- gion, not to speak of the hinterland, is worth. having now that Italy has got it is an obscure question, on which the book: throws some light but not much. There are districts that may be

worth developing ; there are spots along the coast where there is soil, not sand; but It has yet to be proved that the New Romans can re- vive the wealth and glories of the civilisation of ancient days. Centuries of erosion and of drifting sand make the entire region a doubtful asset to the Italian nation.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ALBERTA HARVEST.

An increase of 17,069,942 bushels of grain over that garnered last year, is shown in the esti- mated crop yield for Alberta for the year 1913, in the figures compiled by the Provincial De- partment of Agriculture. The total estimated crop for the present year amounts to 81,535,000 bushels. During the year the crop area placed under cultivation totalled 2,809,310 acres, as compared with 2,391,752 acres during 1912, thus showing an increase in the cultivated area of 417,558 acres. The figures also show a very large increase in the amount of oats and barley grown, indicating that more attention has been given to mixed farming. A decrease is shown in the acreage sown in winter wheat, but this is more than counterbalanced in the yield of spring wheat.

CHAMPION LAYERS.

White Wyandottes Make Another Good Start in Competition.

CHICKENS IN DECEMBER.

We live in an age of laying competitions

- - Hie White Wyandottes, which did so well in the twelve months' contest, ended a few weeks ago, have again started well in a contest began at Sedlescombe. The Utility Poultry Club, dealing with this new competition, reports: "The competition is run in two sections: (1) Small house and run 6ection,- and (2) semi-in- tensive section, and it comprises thirty-two pens of four birds each in each section, each compe-

- titor entering a pen of four birds in each sec- tion. The houses in section 1 are of tongued- grooved matchhoard, measuring 121 f. by Sit. by 6ft. Gin, in front, sloping to Sit. Duck, and -divided in the middle by a, partition ; in each half there are eight birds (two pens of four birds each) to which is allowed a run of 300 square yards. The whole of the birds in section 2 are housed in a large house 401 t. by 201 t., Oft. high in front, sloping to sft. at back, with a run of about 1 acre attached. In unfavourable weather the birds- are confined to the house. In each section the birds are trap-nested; so that individual records are kept. . • The man- ager's repart, for the first four weeks shows that during the period 1,319 eggs have been laid, 767 in section 1 and 552 in section 2.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
WHITE WYANDOTTES LEAD.

Pen 21, white WyandotteS,' holds the lead in both sections, with a score of sixty-six eggs, value Bs. 101 d., in section 1, and a score of seventy eggs, value Bs. 10d., in section 2. At the end of the month fifty-five birds were,lay- ing in section 1, thirty-five in section 2. The 'manager reports that many birds are still back- ward, and that several in section 2 have been affected with colds, which unfortunately have spread, in spite of birds which were found suffering with -cold on arrival being iscilated. Three birds have died during the month, and have been sent to the Veterinary Department of the Board of Agriculture for examination. It appears from the report that the weather for the time of year has not been unfavourable for egg production, though the recent heavy rains and cold morning fogs have been trying to the birds. The following are the five leading pens: Pen 21; breed, White Wyandottes ; small house section, Ist month, eggs ,66, value Bs. 101 d. ; semi-intensive section, Ist mouth, eggs 70, value 8s; 100. 7, White Leghorns, 51, 7s. DECEMBER CHICKENS.

It is still very early indeed to be thinking about chickens, but, says a writer in "Farm and Home," it will pay the farmer well to do so. When one is living under favourable con- ditions this is a practice that may be recom- mended very confidently, for such birds quickly grow into money. Especially is the plan advo- cated this season, for eggs being so extremely scarce .We may reasonably expect 'spring chicken's to be very expensive. The artificial method of rearing must be adopted, and a reli- able brooder should be chosen. Rearing chickens in December and January is by no means child's play, but it is worth the extra labour and expense by reason of the fact that the profit from early birds is so much greater than is the case with those that are brought out later in the season, when the supply is plenti- ful'. I should like to recommend every farmer, save those who live amid unfavourable sur- rour.dings,Ao make the attempt of hatching and rearing a few chickens during the next month or six weeks. Make but the experiment 111,, year, and I am confident that The results \\ more than justify the labour and expense.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
"PEACE AND WAIL"

The November number. of "Peace and War," the new monthly published at 3d., has, among other articles bearing upon one of the most vital questions Of our times, the following: "A Mercenary or a Conscript Army," by Viscount Esher; "The Industrial Danger's of Conscrip- tion," by Sir Robert Hadfield ; "Spheres of In- fluence—and no Great Wars," by Sir Harry Johnston; 'The Clever Mr. Churchill," by Nor- man Angell; "The Racial Evils Of War," by the Hon. Rollo Russell. The policy of "Peace and War" is to advocate the cause of peace, and to give reasons for the faith it proclaims. " We believe in ,peace," say its promoters, " in the sense of the substitution of union for dis- union, Of partnership for rivalry, of comrade- ship for quarrel, only in part because it is a means to the end whereby men can more effi- ciently carry on their war with Nature ;_ much

_ more because it is a means to the end of Mak- ing human intercourse of greater worth and finer quality, more purged of cowardly suspi- cions, of hatreds and misconceptions masquer- ading, as ,virtues, of cruelties and stupidities that darken life. It is a means to this end because the emergence of the finer things de- pends upon a form of human intercourse which can only rest Upon a basis of justice, and that in its turn upon understanding; both of which are in jeopardy .so long as they are subject to the mechanical hazard of physical force." Their intention is to bring before the mind of the European public the significance of a few simple, ascertainable, tangible facts in such fashion that they will frame. unconsciously a working hypothesis of international society, which will lead to deductions sufficiently cor- -rect and sufficiently widespread to do for the political groups what has already been done for the religious groups.

FARM AND GARDEN.

TREATMENT OF GRASS LAND. Field .work is so forward that many farmers at a loss td find suitable employment for

leir men and horses, says " The Times." The winter treatment of grass land is giving

3easonable occupation where the clvintages of riarrowing and manuring are properly • appre- ]iated4:- Example in the meLaanical and iltinurial treatment of 'pastures and meadows is finding an increasing number of followers.

'Me practice has long since emerged from the experimental stage and will one day be a fixed feature in the winter routine. Already many farmers who formerly questioned the ability of grass land to respond to harrows and manures have seen reason to alter their views and have fallen into line with the advanced school of

_ agricultural thought. With caution character- istic. of their class, they have proceeded •in graduated fashion, preferring to shape their plans in the light of personal experience. Changes effected in this way are the more likely to be successful and permanent, and it is a sig- nificant circumstance that few. beginners have regretted the step or halted until ,they had covered the whole of the. available area. Pas- tures are as sensitive to judicious treatment as

_ uleadows, as has been shown by the increased stock-carrying capacity .of land that has been manured.

ROYAL AGRICULTURAL SOCIETY. The report of the council of the Royal Agri- cultural Society presented to the general meet; ing On Wednesday, showed an increase in the membership of 127, compared with last year, the total number of governors and ,members being 10,434, compared with 10,307. During .the year 13 new governors and 700 new mem- bers were elected, but 6 heavy death roll, resig- nations,, and ,other causes , accounted for the difference between these totars and the, net gain. The successful show at Bristol 'yielded a balance of receipts over expenditure of £3,115, a result Upon which the Bristol and'sur- rounding districts are to be congratulated. The arrangements for next year's show at Shrews-, bury are well advanced, and the .particulars supplied foretell an exhibition equal in dimen- sions and representation to that at Bristol.

SWINE FEVER WARNING.

Mr. G. L. Courthope, M.P., Chairman of the. Departmental Committee . appointed by the House of Commons to inquire into the swine fever question, speaking at Wadhurst (Sussex) on Saturday, said that great pressuXe was being put on his committee to advocate the sweeping away of all area restrictions and reliance upon serum only for the eradication of the disease. That was an ereor which had reduced pig farm- ing in the United States of America to an ap- palling state, that country last year losing Over 100,000,000 dollars through the failure of serum. Extensive use of serum only spread the disease, and no successful treatment had yet been dis- covered to stamp out the fever. The country must also 'beware of bringing- pressure to bear on the GoVernment to adopt the serum 'form of treatment, for if the pressure were great enough the Government might give way and the result could only be disastrous.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
POINTS OF TIIE BACON PIG.

The chief points in the pig are those which influence the value of the meat, says a Writer in "Farm and Home," and if breeders took them into full consideration when .selecting their stock they would realise more money than before. The value of a side of bacon depends upon abundance of the best meat and a mini- mum quality of the worst. The inferior meat is at the fore-end, and this is invariably larger in quantity when produced by pigs with short ears, heads and necks. These pigs, too, lay on too much fat, hence the ,fore-end, already in- ferior, is weighted with fat to such an extent that it diminishes ' the value of the:better-por- tions of the side. Well-bred bacon pigs pro- duce a maximum of meat on the belly, loin and hams, the fat not weeding 2 inches in thick- ness on the back, the belly well *caked with lean, the loins and the hams well furnished with tender, lean meat, which is the result of careful feeding and early maturity. A side of bacon should be "sizeable," as it is termed in the trade, neither too large nor too small, but such as will produce useful hams and loins and rasher bacon not too large in size. In these days small meat is preferred to the huge hams and other joints of the past. What is true of bacon is equally true of pork; the butcher wants what will sell—plenty of the best meat and a minimum of that which has no other resort but the sausage machine.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

Turn some old manure into the soil around rhubarb, and afterwards put on a dressing of rank manure for protection. The common sweet briar makes a beautiful hedge for the rose garden.. If left to themselves they make a strong thick hedge in a few years. Clumps of montbretia may be taken•up and divided where they have become too thick. Before • re-planting trench and manure the ground. Many roses die annually from bad cultivation. They are starved and neglected, become eaten up with insects, and expire from exhaustion rl3.s.e than from climatic conditions.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
SOOT AS A FERTILISER.

Soot is not only a most valuable fertiliser, but the cheapest remedy for ridding plants of ' insect pests. It should not be used direct .form the flues, but be kept some months in a .lry place before using. It will clear winter greens of caterpillars. The best way to use it is to get a good sized tin; drill holes in the lid, and dredge the plants with the soot. It will 'keep roses clear of green fly and maggot, and keep chrysanthemums, celery, asparagus and other plants free from insect pests.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
DIGGING AND MANURING.

The basis of all good cultivation of the' soil consists in keeping it dug as deeply as possinle for every crop; preserving it open in texture, by means of a hoe or fork, all through the growing months; and enriching it with manure by which is meant any such fertilising sub- stances as replace what the crops have taken out of it—to a sufficient degree to maintain it in good heart, says the, "Daily Telegraph" garden correspondent. If the soil is good for twenty inches deep it should be double dug' every autumn; in this way the top spit before digging will afterwards be the bottom one, and what was 'previously the bottom one will be brought to the surface. Thus for at least twenty inches -down the soil will be loosened, and rain, air, and sunshine will each help to make it more fertile.

Shallow soils, ten or twelve inches deep only, must be treated differently; the top spit must not be buried, but must remain on the top; while for the depth of another spit below it the soil must be stirred and loosened as well as c:,n be done by the help of a good steel digging fork. Plenty of manure must be worked into it. In time the depth of soil will be so greatly increased that a portion of it may be brought to

the surface. Whichever of these plans is adopted will be found useftil, and a greater space for the roots of plants to grow in will be provided than if the ground be simply eug one spade's depth, as is but commonly the practice. The best gardeners all dig their ground as deeply as possible, and one of the results is that their plants do not surfer „from drought or excessive rains as they otherwise would.

CHARGE AGAINST A ST. HELENS MAN

John Grady, 28, Bold-street, was remanded at St. Helens on a charge of wounding Julia Toole. The Chief Constable said that the man was alleged to have thrown a kettle full of boiling water at Mrs. Toole, and she was in hospital in a serious state, the doctor saying that it would be a fortnight before she could appear in Court.

The Annual Prize Distrihutions at Heathsidey Beamont, Trinity, and Fairfield Schools.

VALUE OF EDUCATED DEMOCRACY.

It was prize week at the various branch Technical Schools in Warrington this week, as is customary prior to the Christmas recess. The year's work was reviewed, and at each the visit- ing members of the Education Committee gave advice and guidance to the pupils. The first gathering took place at Heathside. on Monday evening. Councillor JENKINS, who presided, said he looked back on his school life with the greatest pleasure. He was brought up in a wild, rough country. He had none of the advantages which they had to-day, but he was pleased to tell them, with all the disadvantages they had in his early days, they turned out some good scholars, men of whom they had no need to be ashamed, men who had taken -very high 'posi- tions in life. One of the boys whom he and his school-fellows considered one of the dullest in the school became Premier of South Australia. CAMP FOLLOWERS OF LABOUR.

Mr. J. UPsoN, who was warmly received by the students, said he would like to add his word of praise for the very successful session they had last year, and express his sincere hope that the present 'session would be still more success- ful. He was sure all the students would like to express their sympathy with Mr. T. Eden, their headmaster, in his illness. Pupils went to that, school that they might improve their conditions in life, and improve the material advantages they might get, .so that they might not bbe camp followers in labour's great army.' The Education Committee rejoiced that so large a number of the youths of the town should take life so seriously, that they tried to fit them- selves for the duties of life, and they deserved the highest praise and all the credit that could be given them for going to those schools for that reason. They did not attend those classes alto- gether to increase their value on the labour market, but increase the value of life to them- selves; that was, they came for educational reasons.

The schools led to development of character, and character was power, as well as discipline. They did not merely come under the discipline of the school, but under self-discipline, as their voluntary attendance at the. schools showed. The Education Committee had no power to force them to attend night schools, yet they had come, and had learned to say "No" to the temptations which encouraged participating in the rough games of the street corners. When they learned to say " No " to those minor temp-

tatiOns they had learned to say "No" to the more powerful temptations that come to young men.

Educated Democracy.

The result of the training they received at the schools was that they came to recognise their personal responsibility in life. It was some- times said that the most perfect form of human government was that of an educated democracy, and the most tyrannical form that of an ignorant democracy. In a few years' time the pupils in the night schools would be the men of the town, the electors, and the men to go into the Town Council, and Upon than would fall the responsibility- of having a knowledge which would not be possessed by some of their fellows, and it was in the exercise of that responsibility they would find the value of the training they were now receiving.

Pains after Eating.

Nine times out of ten pains after eating are due to faulty action of the -stomach, liver, or bowels. Some people have to force themselves to eat even the daintiest of dishes because indigestion inevitably extracts its toll of misery and pain. But pains after eating, loss of appetite,. and the distressing symptoms of indigestion, liver trouble, or constipation have been banished, in thousands of cases, by Mother Seigel's Syrup. It is made from more than ten varieties of roots, barks, and leaves, which give to the stomach, liver, and bowels the help they oft-times need. Thirty drops of Mother Seigel's Syrup, taken after each meal for a few days, aid digestion, and will [enable you to enjoy your food without the fear of distressing pains to follow.

AT TRINITY BRANCH.

Father Wilson and the Home.

The distribution of Certificates to the young ladies took place on Wednesday evening at two centres, the first at Trinity Schools and the sectnid at Fairfield Schools. In both cases Father Wilson presided, and Mrs. C. Jackson Holmes presented the prizes. Mr. J. Moore Murray was also present. There was a good assembly of smiling girls at Trinity School when Father Wilson rose to address them. He said they had been learning the arts. and industries of the home. In olden days the school for those arts and industriee, was undoubtedly the home, and those things must always be practised in the home, and all they learned now would be of no great use unless they took their lessons borne with them and tried to put them into practice. He believed the home- was the best place for those arts yet, and it was only the social changes which had taken place, on account of their great indus- trial development and enormous sub-division of work, and the vast millions of capital in the hands of a few, that made it necessary for girls to go out and earn wages, which necessitated their learning those arts in the evening school. The home was no longer the school. It was to be earnestly wished that by the teachings in their evening schools they might gradually build up the traditions of the, homes again. (Ap- plause.)

===== 0002601_19131213_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
MRS. J. HOLMES AND THE WORK.

At this stage Miss Vera Clarke presented Mrs. Jackson Holmes with a bouquet, and Clara Swift handed buttonholes to Mr. J. Moore Murray and Father Wilson. Mrs. JACKSON HOLMES, after expressing her thanks for the bouquet, said that she had been reading the report of their work and exanaina- lions, and she congratulated them that so many who had entered for the latter had passed. She often wondered if all of thepa appreciated the many advantages that were now placed in their way. It was quite common to come across people some years ago who could neither write nor read. She herself had written even love letters for such people when she was young. (Laughter.) Those wha were at the head of affairs, and who took such great interest in working for young people in every degree of life, looked to the young people to do their part as well; not only to do their best to attend even- ing classes, but to carry out in after life in a Practical way all they had been taught.

WALKING ABOUT THE STREETS.

Mr. J. Moons MURRAY said they should take

the advice, and practise in the ho-me what they were taught .in school. To put it in another way, he said-the time would come, probably it had come, when they did not know what to do with, themselves during the, latter part of the week, after spending the first three nights in the evening schools. The first thing.that came into the minds of some of .them was to go out and walk about the- streets. They spent far too 'much time in doing this. Sankey-street, on a Sunday night, was no better than it was ten years ago when he first came to Warrington. He was sorry to say that they were mostly young girls who went there, and who ought to be at home.

WIDNES MOTOR 'BUS LOSSES.

Alderman Owens, at Widnes Town Council on Tuesday, reported that the Motor 'Bus Com- nalttee.had underspent-RB6, but that was on the surface only.' The net loss on the half-year was actually £1,231, or £47 per week. It would take another six months to wipe off the debt on the garage, and then they would have a chance to breathe more freely. If, however, they could not conceive some plan for remedying this ex- pensive system they would be confronted with a,loss year by year of 2d. to 20. in the Motor 'buses had, however, come to stay, but any 'buses Purchased in the future would have to be purchased out of revenue. Councillor Welding said the Motor 'Bus Com- mittee were not as despondent as Alderman Owen. They had improved the position during the last six months, and he believed that in the next 13ix months they would reduce the loss by one-half.

HORSES ELECTROCUTED AT ST. HELENS. William Alker, a farmer, was awarded £Bl and costs against the St. Helens Corporation, in the Liverpool County Cmirt, in respect of the death of two horses which were killed as, the result of a curious accident. It appeared that, following upon stormy weather, a telephone wire came into contact ,with one of the defend- ants' electric tramway wires and, when charged with a high current of electricty, fell upon the horses and electrocuted them.

WOMEN'S QUESTIONS.

Imitation Furs Can Never Compare

QUEEN DISLIKES THE TANGO

The King and Queen, like the German Em- peror, do not approve of the "new dancing." Her Majesty dislikes extremes, whether in matters of dress or manners, and her favourite dances are those of the Victorian period. She is fond of the quadrille and the waltz, with an occasional polka, but the. lancers seldom, if ever, appear on Royal programmes. It is, therefore, not surprising that their Majesties should have expressed ,a decided opinion that the Tango is not suitable for ballrooms, and

neither it, nor any of the negroid importations from across the Atlantic, will be permitted at Court, or at any of the great houses when Royalty is present. The -Tango is not an American dance. Attempts have been made to trace it back to ancient times, but it is really of Spanish origin. For the time being it has captivated all classes of society, and, despite Royal disfavour, it seems likely to carry every- thing before it in social entertainments this winter. It even threatens the fancy dress ball, notwithstanding the fascination which, the op- portunity of "dressing up" has for the terpsi- chorean enthusiast:

The Picture Ball.

Princess Louise, Duchess of Argyll, occupied

the Prince of Wales' box at the Picture Ban given in the Albert Hall fa aid of the invalid kitchens of Lbndon. A most interesting group was Lady Constance Hatch's Cosinay minia- tures, the effect\of which was much heightened by a black velvet screen, against which, in diamond studded frames, the heads appeared. Lady Constance wore a dress that belonged to an ancestress who died a hundred years ago. Baroness d' Erlanger's pictures from mosaics of the first to the tenth centuries, representing saints and angels, in dresses of Vivid colour- ings, were very much admired, for they were a unique 'conception and beautifully carried out. Assyrian, Egyptian, Greek and Boman schools of painting were Most effectively portrayed in the form of a frieze of black velvet.

Christmas Shopping. The spirit of Christmas is abroad again, and the shops overflow with a bewildering variety of novelties and the most tempting presents. Only to look at them is A sheer delight, although one may be conscious that possession will never be ours. Wise people are accomplishing their Christmas shopping betimes this year, and the shops have done their best to be in readiness. There is a' fashion even in Christmas gifts, and every .year some article or accessory looms largely before customers. This season, thither, clear or clouded is accorded a place of honour, more especially amber beads, and these are often of barbaric proportions. They form an acceptable gift, and are most effective in con-

trast with several shades now much donned. Coral leads are favoured, too, so are beads of Chinese jade, in pink or shaded green and white, but the latter are more expensive than amber. Buckles 'for evening shoes are popu- lar gifts, and fans and gloves are as much sought after as ever before. Quite a safe selec- tion is one of the. many useful and beautiful ,bags. There are capacious bags for household shopping, and those of gold and silver mesh for the afternoon. The dinner party and the theatre are also amply provided for, and bags

for these functions are of the daintiest materials, brocade, and especially moire, being much in evidence.

The Decline of the Black Hat. A great French artist is of opinion that Eng- lishwomen should wear large hats, and that the small toque and cap, with their bizarre, up- standing feathers, for Which there is such a vogue at present, are not suited to the English face. But the large hat is still worn by many women ,whom it suits, and in the spring it will doubtlegs come back again, for people arc already tiring of the tiny hat. The all black velvet hat has evidently had .its day, and is being replaced with a confection that matches the dress worn with it. The fantastic mount also shows signs of having reached the zenith of its popularity, for milliners are using one or two ' large, but beautiful flowers, carelessly fastened to the side of the hat, on a number of the latest models. The flowers are generally of velvet, beautifully made, the preference being given to gardenias, Christmas roses and pansies.

Magpie Suits in Duvetyn. Black duvetyn has quite replaced velvet for the moment in Paris, and is making headway ' on this side of the Channel. It has a shiny sur- face, but less so than velvet, it also hangs more gracefullysthan the latter fabric, and therefore lends itself admirably to the draperies of coats, and the folds of skirts. For tailored suits it is just now extremely popular, and when trimmed with skunk is at its best. A white waistcoat, also of duvetyn, adorned with.black buttons, is a favourite relief to one of these costumes. Coats and skirts are this winter built frequently of striped and fancy materials. Batine, more especially in green, striped with black, has met with much favour. Plain fabrics are equally popular, and diversity in materials and styles is the order of the day. Never has there been such an unlimited choice. Waists may be high, normal or abnormally low; coats short or fairly -long, and endless in variety of styles and decoration.

Imitation Furs.

The pelts of the hare, rabbit,, cat and some species of rats and weasels are all impressed into the service of Dame Fashion, but though the skill of the furrier is manifested' in the dressing and dyeing of these pelts, making them quite presentable, t'they cannot compare with the real furs they are lintended to imitate, and always look what they are. For young girls, the necklet is a most sensible, as well as siliart idea, for it takes so little fur that a really good one may be indulged in without, extravagance. Fortunately for women of small means, who yet like to look smart and pp-to-date, a com- bination of velvet and fur is highly favoured this winter. A long stole of We former trimmed with ii narrow band of, good fur, with a big muff to match, is a desirable possession, and " goes " \\ ith any dress. Even the severest coat' and skirt can be.worn at an afternoon function, if relieved and smartened by one of these hand- some-looking sets. 'Chiffon or net ruffles arc' often added to the muffs, and are kept in place by pretty buckles, small in size.

SOME CHRISPIAS JEATING.

Dreams, Visions and Stories of the Spirit World.

"MILLIONS OF SPIRITUAL CREfITLIQES WALK THE EARTH UNSEEN, BOM WHEN WE WAKE aND WHEN WE SLEEP."

I may tell you at the onset that I know noth- ing whatever about the bourne from which, as Shakespeare says, no traveller returns, to tel; us as to its locality, or to give us any particulars as tà. its composition; but while we have no direct evidence of modern times that there is such a world, we have had sundry mysterious signs, or, as I should pre- fer to term them, indications, that there is a certain affinity betwn the spirit that inhabits our bodies, and the ether or space cvith aich we are surrounded, that to a thoughtful and highly sensitive person is very apt to carry him out of himself—or herself. . There are very few families who have not had certain signs or imagined they have had warn- ing of a Certain great event that was about to happen, and which has given them a presenti- ment as to what they might expect. Sir Walter Scott, in his book of demonology and witch- craft, gives us some very interesting details of this character. We are told of a very singular case which happened before the time of the telegraph or steam engine.

about the time he heard her voice she was call- ing him; she was very ill and hardly expected to live.

health depends. Every white corpuscle that swims in our veins is now declared to be the armed knight .of Life for ever on the look-out ,f or the microbe fiend of Death. Day and night, sleeping and waking, the white knights of Life are constantly on the alert, for on their vigi- lance hangs our existence. Sometimes, however, the • iniading microbes come in, not in com- panies, but in battalions, and we die. But recent researches into the nature and "constitution of our mental and spiritual parts would suggest almost inconceivable possibili- ties as to our own nature; they leave us as- tounded-on the brink of a new world, of which until recently most of us .were unaware. • A very curious instance is told in which a vision or dream was instrumental in preventing a murder. It is recorded of a daughter of Mrs. Rutherford, at Ederton, the granddaughter of Sir Walter Scott. This lady dreamed more than once that her mother had been murdered by a black servant. She was so much upset by this that she returned home, and to her great astonishment, and not a little to her dismay, she met on entering the house, the very black servant she 'had seen in her dreams. He had been engaged in her absence. She prevailed upon a gentleman to watch in an adjoining room during the .following night. About three o'cloCk in the morning the 'gentleman heard footsteps on the stairs. He came out and met the servant carrying a quantity' of coals. On being asked where he was going with them, be answered 'confusedly that he was going to mend the mistress's fire, which, at three o'clock in the morning in the middle of summer, was evi- dently impossible. On further investigation a strong knife was found hidden in the coals. The lady escaped, but the man was subsequently hanged for murder, and before his execution he confessed that he intended to assassinate Mrs. Rutherford.

DO SPIRITS WALK AMONGST US?

The spirits of men do not die, they still live and walk abroad amongst us. It was a fine and true thought, uttered by Mr. Disraeli in the House of Commons, on the death of Richard Cobden, "that he was one •dl those men who, though not present, were still members of that House, who were independent of dissolution, of the caprice of constituencies, and even of the course of time."

There is no need to be a Pagan in order to people the world with invisible denizens, with which our forefathers peopled every hill and glen and every flowery Mead. No doubt there has been, and is, a great deal of superstition and nonsense talked about ghosts, and a very creepy and weird atmosphere has plagued the whole regiOn in which these ideas have been in vogue, but these should not keep the resolute soul from its appointed ,path; and the path of all mankind is to try all things, to prove all things, and to hold fast to that which is good. A very curious and striking instance is told of the father of a son who had sailed in a vessel called the "Strathmore," an emigrant ship out- ward bound from the Clyde. The father saw one night in a dream the ship floundering amid the waves, and saw that his son, with some others, had escaped safely to a desert island near which the wreck had taken place. .He was so much impressed with this vision that he wrote to the owner of the "Strathmore," telling him what he had seen. His information was scouted, but after awhile the " Strathmore " was over.-de and the owner got very uneasy. Day followed day, and still no tidings of the missing ship. Then, like Pharaoh's butler, the owner remembered his sins, and one day hunted up the letter describing the vision. It supplied at least a theory to .account for the vessel's dis- appearance. All outward bound vessels were requested to look for any survivors on the island indicated in the vision. These orders being obeyed, the survivors of the " Strathmore " were found exactly where the father had seen them. Taken in connection with other instances of a similar nature, what can be said of excepting that it almost necessitates the supposition that onr spirits have a latent power of Which as yet we are not conscious?

Air. Ed. H. Lipsitt, of Tralee, tells the follow- ing curious , experience: When. quite a boy, lie says, some ten years .of age, he happened to sleep 'one night in the same ropin with a young friend 'of about his own age. There existed a very strong sympathy between them. He says lie got up very early and went out for a short walk, leaving his friend fast asleep in bed. He went in the direction of a well-known lake in the district. After gazing for some moments at the silent water, he espied a large black dog making for him. He turned his back and fled, the dog following him for some distance. His boots then being in a bad condition, one of the soles came of/ in the flight; however, he got away unmolested by the dog; but lie was very Much amazed. on entering the room where his young friend. , who was just rubbing his eyes and yawning, related to him his adventure, word for word, 4escrilAg even the colour of the (log and the very boa '(the right one) the sole of which gave, .waY•" *. Sir Walter Scott . says:

There is something in this ancient superstition Which, errlng as it is, our fancy loves!

Wild as is the theory that, some of the stories may suggest, it is less fantastic than it would have appeared to our grandfathers a hundred years ago, if they had been asked to believe that at the end of the 19th century portraits would be taken by the sun, that conversation would be carried on across a distance of thousands of miles, that a ray of "light would be made the agent for transmitting the human thoughts across an abyss which no wire had ever spanned, and that by a simple mechanical arrangement which can be carried in a man's hand, it would be possible. to. reproduce the words, voieb and accent of the dead. The photograph, the tele- graph, the telephone, the phonograph were all more or less latent in, what seemed to our fore- fathers; the folly of 'Benjamin Franklin. Who knows but that in telepathy we may have the faint foreshadowing of another latent force, which 'may be destined to cast into the shade even the marvels of electrical science? The fairy tales of science are innumerable, and, like the fairy tales of old romance,. they thefa arsee fascinated el lacking horrible.e kti the imagination Oiifnm engnarttiimon, yearstehvee tragicnothing nela di least so aenot imaginative of men as the theory of disease which t • forms every drop of blood in our bodies into the lists of which phagocyte and tehfin the ah microbe wage the mortal strife on whieh our

DREAMS THAT CAME TRUE.

The assassination of Mr. Percival in the lobby of the House of Commons was foreseen in the Minutest details by John Williams, a Cornish mine manager, eight or nine days before the assassination took plape. Three times over he dreamed that he saw a small man dressed in a blue coat and white waistcoat enter the lobby of the House of Commons, when immediately another person, dressed in a snuff-coloured coat, took a pistol from under his coat and shot the little man in the left breast. -On asking who the little man was, he was informed that it was Percival, Chancellor of the Exchequer. He was so much impressed by the dream that he consulted his friends as to the desirability of warning Mr. Percival. Unfortunately they dissuaded him, and on May 13th the news came that Percival had been killed on the 11th. Some time afterwards, when he saw a picture of this scene, it reproduced all the thrilling details of this thrice dreamed vision. Some people would call this a coincidence, and I have heard others venture the opinion that the spirit is 'capable of wandering from the body while it sleeps, and while this gentleman slept, his spirit had, strange to say, Wandered in the precincts of the House of Commons. My grandfather, who was a good old local preacher among the Independent Methodists, tells •a very funny story of a dream or vision he once had. He says* it was the night of the Walking Day, and he found, when he got home, that he had lost his purse. He went .to bed and dreamt he was standing on his head against a stump, and while in that position he saw his purse drop out of his pocket and fall at the foot of the post. He was so deeply impressed by this dream that when he got up the nelt morn- ing he went to the place and found the identical spot where the stump or post was, and, on examining round it, sure enough, there was his purse intact, with the money all right. Dreams and visions were vouchsafed to our forefathers. Why should they not be given to us to-day? We are told that the world is getting more materialised and practical every day, but in the face of 'this assertion, science is demonstrating to us that there are elements and agencies that we cannot understand, and that makes' Us stand awestricken at the un- fathomable mysteries of the unseen world.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
SOUND TRANSMISSION.

Marconi, the great discoverer of the wireless telegraphy, tells us that in the very ether or air we breathe there exists a latent power that can transmit sound through a mountain range, or across a trackless sea, and who knows but what this power, which he can harness and make to do his will, may not be the same power which Madam Blavatsky and her followers say is capable of carrying thought from people who have an affinity one for the other through space of almost illimitable expanse? Those of us who may not have passed through the transforming stage of a sound conversion cannot realise the beautiful divine feeling which Pervades the soul Of one who has been soundly converted, and very few can explain why the countenance of a person gets transformed from a hard commonplace to a radiant .attractiveness that was never there before. I have heard people explain this transformation by saying that the affinity of the known with the unknown works this miraculous change, and who is there of us can gainsay this? A relation of mine, who was studying for the ministry in Manchester, was reading one night in his study. It would be about 10 o'clock, when. he fancied' he heard his name called by a very familiar voice, which he at once recognised as the voice of a dear little lady friend. He was so impressed with this that he at once lay down his book-, caught the 11 o'clock train from i'ionchester, and Came on here, to find that
