TO PROVE LIFE AFTER DEATH.

When we come to visions pure and simple, one of the best known leading cases is that re- corded by Lord Brougham, who was certainly one of the hardest-headed persons that ever lived, a .Lord Chancellor trained from his youth up to weigh evidence. The story is. given as follows in the, first volume of Lord Brougham's memoirs: "A most remarkable thing happen to me, so remarkable that I must tell the from the beginning. After I left the High Selx 1 I went with a friend to attend classes at University. There were no divinity classes, but we.: frequently in our walks discussed niany grave Subjects, amongst others the immortality of the soul and the future state. This subject and the possibility of the dead appearing to the living, were subjects of much speculation, and we actually committed, the •folly of drawing up .an agreement written with our blood to the effect that whosoever died first should appear to the other, and thus solve any doubts we Dad entertained of the life after' death. After we had finished our studies, my friend went out to India, where he had got an appointment in the Civil Service. He seldom wrote me, and after the lapse of a number of years I had almost for- gotten hixn. One day I was taking a warm bath, and while lying in it, and enjoying the com- forts of the heat, I turned my head round, looking towards the °hair upon which I had deposited my clothes. On the chair sat my friend G. . . . looking calmly at me. 11Ow I got out of the bath I know • not, but on recov- ering my senses I found myself sprawling on the floor. The apparition or whatever had taken the likeness of my friend had vanished." This vision had produced such a shock that he says he had no desire to talk about it; so sfronglY was he affected by it that he wrote down the date, December 19th., "No doubt," he proceeds, "I had fallen asleep and the ap- pearance presented so distinctly before my eyes was a dream, I cannot for a moment doubt. I recollected quickly enough our old discussion, and the bargain we had made. I could not dis- charge the thought from my mind that 'my friend was dead, and that his appearance to me was to be received as a proof that there was a future state. This was on December 19th, 1799." On October, 1862, Lord Brougham adds, as a postscript, "I have just been copying from my journal the account of this strange dream, and now. to finish the story begun 60 years ago. Soon after my return to Edinburgh there arrived a letter from India announcing my friend's death, and stating that he' died on December 19th, 1799." • Shakespeare says:

There are more things in heaven and earth, Horatio, Than are dreamt of in your philosophy.

WILLIAM FURNESS Fern Lea, Great Sankey.

CHILD DRINKERS.

Amazing Revelations About a Warrington Public-House.

A MAXIMUM FINE IMPOSED.

Amazing revelations w6re made in Warring- ton Police Court on Tuesday about drinking amongst boys and girls. The police found these comparative children not only drinking, but, along with grown-up men and women, acting in a noisy mann4r. As a result of the police raid, the maximum penalty was imposed on George Eastwood, licensee of the Crown and Sceptre Buttermarket-street, who was charged with permitting 'riotous conduct on his licensed Premises on Saturday, November 22nd. Mr. A. Browne appeared for the defendant, who was unable to attend through illness, and Mr. W. 11. J. Jenkins represented the interests of Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co., the pro- prietors. THE CHIEF'S 'STORY.

The Chief Constable stated tlukt those pro- ceedings arose out of the 'fad that periodical visits were paid to the licensed-premises in the town. Inspector Forster, in company with P.C. Bennett, on the date in question ware paying these periodical visits to a number of the licensed • premises, and at 9 p.m. visited the Crown and Sceptre in Buttermarket-street. They entered the premises by the side door leading from Bank-street and founil the passage packed with men and women standing there drinking. They then visited the billiard room awl found this .densely packed, principally by youths and girls 16 or 17 years of age. In one -part of the room was a notorious character, holding the hand of another man, and those two were endeavouring to sing, in fact the whole of the company, in the room was endeavouring to sing. To use the words of the, Inspector, the noise was "most hideous." The police visited other parts of the house, and there the same condition existed, and the Inspector would tell them that a great percentage of the persons on the premises were intoxicated, • although they could not swear thy were drunk, but certainly they were. under the influence of drunk.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
LANDLADY RESENTS.

Inspector Forster saw Mrs. Eastwood am] asked tO see her husband. She stated that her husband was ill in bed, and she resented the

BABY THERE FOR AN HOUR.

The woman was questioned as to how long she had been on the premises, and she replied she had been there sixty minutes, and admitted having had drink. The police found exactly the same state of affairs prevailing as on the previous occasion.

- The statement of the Chief Constable was corroborated by Inspector 'Forster and P.C. Bennett.

Mr. Browne asked. Inspector Forster if on the day in question the Widnes football team visited Warrington, and on receiving an answer in the affirmative, intimated that that was his .only question.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
A LAMEN7ABLE CASE.

Mr. Browne stated that he was not, going to dispute what Inspector Forster or Constable Bennett had said, and he ,had no doubt that Ins-peCtor Hodgkiss, if he was called, would give a similar truthful story. It was a most lament- able case. Mr. Eastwood had kept that house for six years; and the Filecutters' Arms, in Fennel-street, for three years before that, and there had 'been .no .conviction or serious com- plaint made up to this time. Defendant had

been suffering from chronic rheumatism for nine months, with the result that he could not get downstairs only on rare occasions, and thus he was unable to conduct the business himself, and he left it in charge of his wife and a man named.Fairbrother. It was entirely against the defendant's wish that anything of this kind should have taken place. They had heard the police evidence 'that the house was so densely packed that :they could not get through, and the billiard room was also densely packed. They had also heard that the snug and the parlour were packed. He submitted that that day was not an ordinary occasion. The Widnes and Warrington football match was played on that day, and a very large number of persons came from Widnes, with the result that every public- house in town was packed. The place absolutely got beyond the control of Mrs. Eastwood and Mr. Fairbrother. The billiard-room would seat 50 or 60 people, the. passage would hold a large number, the parlour would seat 20 or 30, the snug 12 or 15, and every passage was packed, and he asked what was a woman to do under those circumstances.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
"LOST HER TEMPER."

Unfortunately she lost her temper. It was absolutely impossible for any landlady or land- lord to properly conduct a house which was invaded by something like 120 or 150- people, wherr„ perhaps, on other Occasions they would only find something like 20, 30 or 40 present. It Was only a rare occasion that the house was in such a state, and the owners would see that there was no repetition. Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins said that ale owners by no means approved of conduct of this kind, and as Mr. Browne had intimated, steps would at once-be made to see that defendant did not con- duct any licensed house the property of the owners, whom he represented. That was set- tled. The character of the proceedings in that house on the night in question were such that it would be impossible for tlit owners for one moment to countenance, and they understood that the conduct of a licensed house should be carefully safeguarded. There should be no neg- lect Or inattention on the part of the tenant which could bring about a state of things, as in that case. He intimated that any wish from the Bench would receive the most careful attention of the owners.

Roberts. stated that they felt the case was as bad as .it possibly could be, and they failed to see any redeeming point in it. Steps should have been taken long ago to make proper supervision pos- sible. They had decided to inflict the maximum penalty of £lO ;Ind costs.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 13, 1913.

"ft Merry Christmas!"

—from the Shops.

How the Business Part of the Town is Providing an Arabian Night Entertainment.

NO TIME TO BE DISMAL.

"Dark and dull night, flue hence away, And give the honour to this day,. That sees December turned to May.

Why does the chilling winter's morne Smile like a field beset with corn? Or smell like to a meade new-shorne. Thus on the sudden. Come and see The cause Nsilly things thus fragrant be." (Herrick.)

Yes! Come and see all the charming thing, which are displayed in the beautiful shops Warrington! Come and seo pretty things whic: will delight,your hearts and eyes. Come ii hundreds, do not come alone, bring your uncles cousins, aunts, and don't leave behind tin children, for they will draw your attention to scores of pretty things which attract their eyes, until at last you may imagine that you are liv- ing in the wonderful realm of Arabian Nights' Land. But no matter, come! That is what we ask of you. Come and see! Oh! what shops of toys! What delightful displays of Christmas cheer! What a glow of warmth emanates from the shop windows just now! What feasts, in store for bright-eyed boys and girls! And the empty pockets for father and mother! Well! even that is quite all right, and 'tis mom* well spent to give the bairns a good time once a .year. One can't be a skinflint at Christmas! We have no room for any snarling Scrooge in our midst, and we have the' 'bright lights and glorious colours peeping at us from all the shop windows in town, and wishing us—as far as lights and colours can wish us—a real, cheery, good, old- fashioned Merry Christmas! And we mean to have it, too! Who was it who suggested the idea of the year 'l3 being a black, dismal, dole- ful year? I don't know, but if that narrow- minded Mt. Pessimist and his rival, Mr. Opti- mist, took a walk round the town and stopped here and there, arrested by the display in the windows, I fancy he would stop and .say %to his friend:

"Old 'l3 means to finish up brightly! He might have kept up his reputation and died dismally." "Fiddle-sticks," says Mr. Optimist, "there's no time to be dismal. Just come with me and I'll show you a thing or two." " Where are you going?" "Into the shops, of course. I'm going to have a gay time and spend all my money. Come along." " No, thanks. I'm not coming. Spend my money! Catch me."

"Look here, Mr. Pessimist, of Mess'rs. Skin- flint, ‘Seurface, and Co., dealers in Christmas cheer destroyers, didn't you want to see our shops?" "Of course I did."

"Well, wait until you've seen our Christmassy town and then you'll change your name double quick."

CHRISTMAS PARCELS.

The London and North-Western Railway and

Quick Transit.

The London and North-Western Railway Com- pany have made complete arrangements for the collection, quick transit, and prompt delivery of Christmas parcels in all the principal towns on their system, and all parts of Wales, Scot- land and Ireland. Special " parcel " trains will be run, and through vans will also be attached to the principal passenger trains for the accom- modation of the traffic, and additional delivery and collection services by parcel vans and carts between the receiving offices and the railway stations will be in operation during Christmas and the preceding week. Senders are advised thitt as, an _extra precaution a duplicate address label should be enclosed in each parcel, so that the name and address of the consignee may be ascertained in the event of the outside label becoming detached or defaced.

CHRISTMAS TRAINS. Provision Made for our Comfort by the L. and N.-W.

An advertisement on this, page gives de- tails of the arrangements made for travelling at Christmastide to and from the parts of , the kingdom; covered by the L. and N.-W. system, which means practically the whole of the kingdom.

THE EMA.MINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 13, 1913.

BIRTHS. MARRIAGES. DEATHS.

attend to their own affairs that has made the Empire what it is to-day, and the withholding of which from Ireland has kept that sister nation poor, disconsolate, and the victim of office seekers, and the worst class of faction mongers.' The justifications to rebel given by , Mr. JOYNSON-HICKS might be given in favour of a rebellion by the Nationalists, but'they are in a tremendous majority, and they ask, in a Constitutional way—that is, by their votes— that they shall be treated justly and fairly, and so given a chance to show what a really united Empire may mean. It would be too tedious to deal in this man- ner with all the false issues raised by Mr. JoYNsoN-Hicks. But he made a point of the benefits Ireland would get at the extiense of the English taxpayer. We may take it for granted that Ireland will pay her share for any services rendered. She usually has done so. We suppose .Mr. JOYNSON-HICKS is not so un- informed as not to know that a Royal Com- mission report, issued as recently as 1896, found that the Act of Union imposed upon Ireland burdens greater than she could bear, and that LORD MACDONNELL estimated the financial injustice to Ireland for the century to ,be £300,000,000. If Mr. JOYNSON-HICKS did not know this, we make him a present of the fact, and ask him to blush at the mean suggestion he served out for the delectation of his Saturday night Audience. If LORD MACDONNELL, on financial adjustment, is not found satisfying, may we recall to his'memory fhat Mr. BALFOUR, speaking in the comparatively calm times of 1898, for the Unionist party, said: "There was a time, n unhappy time, when the British aPar- liament thought. it well employed in crushing out Irish manufacture in the interest of the British producer. It was a cruel, and it has proved a stupid policy." Finally, may we re- mind Mr. JOYNSON-HICKS that ,his own party offered to give Home Rule, and that Tory news- papers were set to work to extol ilk virtues in exchange for the saving of the House of Lords. But the Lords were beyond redemption, and

Home Rule has had to conic through the ordinary channel, the party fight. Constitu- tionally, that fight is sound, three-fourths of Ireland demanding it, and a majority of about 100 in the House of Commons being anxious to accord it.,

===== 0002601_19131213_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
TARIFF REFORM RECKONING APPLIED TO HOUSING.

Tory speakers are stating that the shortage of houses in the rural districts has ,been caused by the action of the present Government. They state that when the Tories werp in power. over 100,000 cottages were built Per annum, while, when the Liberals Were in poWer, only 10,000 were provided. Many points have to be borne in mind,i such as the fact that the more insani- tary dwellings are Cleared away , the smaller win appear • the ITICIVO.,SC:on the same number of new houses erected.

Dealing with the figures themselves, we find that in the following years the increase in the number of houses under a £2O rateable value .11 each year was as follows :

Financial Dwelling-

Year!i

Year. 1900-01 19011.02 1902-03 1903-04 1904-05 1905-06 1906-07 1907-08 1908-09 1909-10 1910-11 1911-12

Houses. ... 5,612,000 ... ... 5,703,000 ... ... 5,791,000 ... ... 5,833,000 ... ... 5,935,000 ... 6,048,000 ... ... 6,128,000 ... ... 6,208,000 ... ... 6,280,000 ... ... 6,368,000 ... ... 6,379,000 ... ... 6,459,000 ...

Increaae.

91,000 88,000 41,000 102,000 113,000 80,000 80,000 72,000 88,000 11,000 80,000

To take 10,000 as being the average Liberal year, and 100,600 the average Tory year, is a piece of gross juggling with figures. It is the old Tariff Reform dodge of selecting the best figure for your own purpose, and the worst figure for your opponent's. Thus it will be seen that in 1903 the increase was only 41,000, although it was a Tory year, while in 1912 the increase was 80,000, though it was a Liberal year. The year in which the figure went down to 10,000 was that immediately succeeding the rejection of .the Budget by the Lords, and the consequent financial chaos which resulted.

PASSIYG WEEK.

MR. CROSFIELD SUCCEEDS LORD AVEBURY Our congratulations to Mr. A. H. Crosfield, who has been specially sought out by the National Association of Grocers' Assistants to fill the President's chair, rendered vacant by the death of Lord Avebury. Mr. Crosfield's experi- ence in business matters and his keen interest in all movements for the betterment of the con- ditions of the workers, combine to make his appointment a very fitting one. Certainly to be selected to follow Lord Avebury is no small com- pliment, and Mr. Crosfield will see in it the measure of the confidence the Association have in him.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
COKE.

At the meeting of the Council last week, Councillor Osborne .called attention to a reso- lution of the Gas Committee "that the Deputy- Chairman and Alderman Tinnion be appointed to act with the General Manager in disposing of the surplus stock of coke." It appears that there has been a difficulty in finding customers, and Councillor Osborne suggested that greater facilities should be afforded the poorer people to get it. At the present time, a person cannot purchase less than 5 cwt., and he has to arrange for his own carting. Coun- cillor Osborne's contention is that as all Corporation Departments e7iist for the con- venience of all classes of ratepayers, and as it is possible to get a pennyworth of gas, it should be equally easy to get a pennyworth of coke, if required. Further than that, he contends that depots should be provided in different parts of the town, so that the difficulty of get- ting the coke home might be obviated. It appears to be a reasonable request, and one that, especially at this time of the year, would be a great boon to those people who cannot take, for various reasons, a large supply. There should then be no difficulty in disposing of the stock.

NEW CURATES.

"An Old Journalist" writes As already announced, a new curate is ex- pected at St. Paul's in .the New Year, and an- other for the Parish Church is to be ordained deacon on the 21st inst. One who has gone through it all says that few experiences can be more trying., Suffer it short extract from his detailed account: ." At the most solemn dai of his life, the day of his ordination, the young man finds himself one of a 'considerable body upon whom 'the laying on of hands' takes place. The stately service in the cathedral is of considerable length, and the strain upon the Physical endurance of the candidate is very great. When all is over, he is hurried away to the parish to which he has been appointed, and, almost before he knows where he is, he finds himself in .a state of breathless astonishment in the presence of a crowd of people whom he has never seen before, and whom by the book he is addressing as his dearly beloved brethren.' The 'dearly beloved are all there, and alas! nearly all of them are in their most active critical mood." And ,the writer goes on .to- " pitch into 'em" and "show 'em up," even as they "let the new curate have it." No doubt there is a good deal of truth in Ills rather strong stA.cnients-•-villieri I do not now- reproduce.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
Atfi ,

"FIRE, AND BE DAMNED."

Mr. JOYNSON-HICKS, M.P., visited Warrington last Saturday, and, speaking in the name of Sir EDWARD CARSON, the uncrowned king of a small corner of Ulster, demonstrated, in twelve or fourteen lines, much better than we could clo in a column, the desperate plight into which alleged "Unionists" find themselves. Personify- ing CARSON and apostrophising the PRIME MINISTER, this shining light of the Tory party said:

"Let the Army do its duty. lam still going to do mine. I face the Prime Minister to tell him, 'Bring up your Army, bring up your batteries, bring up your Navy. Behind me thee are 90,000 men.in Ulster. Behind them there are half a million of women and children. Behind them is the whole Unionist party in the House of Commons. Above them is the Lord God of battles. In His name and under His protection I say to you, the Prime Minister, with your arms, with your batteries, 'fire—fire if you dare, fire and be damned."

The sight of Orangemen doing all these things in the name and under the protection of the Almighty savours too much of blasphemy to oall for serious treatment. But we may be excused if we pause to say a few words upon the more mundane, ot the more Ulster, portion of a characteristic outburst by a representative of the gentlemanly party. To .our mind it appears, and we have no doubt that all thought- ful men and women will share our opinion, that the last ditch has, in reality been reached when, for lack of argument, and in sheer annoyance at finding himself in a hopeless plight, a man gives way ..to cursing his opponent. The meeting in question began with song, "the red, white and blue"—which "Unionists" endeavour to appropriate ,for poli- tical purpos/es—being pressed once more into the service of a party who are ever ready to raise the cry of danger to the Empire, and to wrap the Union Jack around a bureaucracy now fighting, fortunately, its last fight for its life. We shall have a nobler use for the Union Jack when the people of Ireland have been indeejd united, when the men who dare to separate a people into sections, and hypo- critically dub themselves "Unionists," . have been driven beyond even the cursing stage, and finally routed. Between the singing and the swearing points on Saturday night the case against Home Bute was not presented as an argument on points; it was presented as a case of irrelevancies, unsubstantiated statements following up6n false premises, and it broke down in the pitiful exhibition which Mr. JoyNsox-HicKs would call his peroration.

One thing emerged clearly from a riot of strong and abusive language. Many English- men have been careful about saying, in so many words, that they are in favour of civil war. It was once a dangerous attitude to assume, and may be again. But Mr. JOYNSON- McKs has no misgivings. He recited the con- cocted grievances of Ulster, with which we will Shortly deal, and then said: "I tell you that those causes not merely justify; they demand rebellion on the part of the people of Ulster." Fortunately this champion of Constitutionalism, who favours a disappointed minority appearing in arms against the KING'S peace, gave us his reasons, which we can submit to examination. lie said that rebellions might be justified on one of four grounds—treachery, cruelty, op- pression, and violation of the Constitution— and all these were present here. For the

Purposes of our argument we assume that the King, his Ministers, and the State, are, as in fact they must be in a Constitutional country, one, and it is against the State that the rebel- lion must take place. When was treachery practised by the State towards the Orangemen of •Ulster? Since 1885 the Liberal party have promised Ireland this measure of justice, and the fact that it is to be given at the first oppor- tunity that the pledge can be redeemed cannot constitute treachery towards the minority, who have had quarter of a century's notice of what to expect, and have, as a matter of fact, been talking about fighting and being in the right for the whole of that long period. Who has been cruel to the Orangemen of Ulster? Who has ever oppressed Orangemen? These are questions that are answered in the asking. But we suppose that Mr. JorNsoN-Hicks's clinching "argument" was that the Home Rule Bill is a violation of the Constitution. It is here the gentleman is on the thinest of his very thin ice. The rock bottom nof Con- stitutional government is that the majority must—in the absence of cruelty, oppression and treachery—have its way, provided for in Con- stitutional method. 'W'hat have we here? For centuries the majority of the people of Ireland —not minority, be it remembered—nave been fighting against the cruelty, the oppression, too bare-faced to be treacherous, studiously prac- tised upon them both by Orangemen and British politicians. PROFESSOR DICEY, a

Unionist, has summarised the Act by which, a century ago, it was sought finally to curb the people of Ireland to other people's selfish ends. "The Act of Union," he says, "was an agreement which, could it have been referred to a Court of Law, must have at once been cancelled as a contract hopelessly tainted with fraud and corruption." Since that fraud was perpetrated upon Ireland in the sacred name of the Union Jack, these Constitutional gentle- men have tried to hold their ill-gotten ground: But since the people had the vote placed at their disposal, and Constitutional methods have increasingly taken the place of violence, the people have advanced, and they now "demand"—to use Mr. JON:NSON-lIICKS'S word in circumstances where right, and not arro- gance, permits of its use—that freedon-, to

Strategy. A farmer went to the home of a 'country doctor in the village late one night, and be- sought him to come at once to a distant farm- house.

The medicine man hitched up his horse, and they drove to the farmer's home. Upon their arrival the farmer said:

"How much is your fee, doctor?" "Half a guinea," said the physician in sur- prise.

"Thank you," said the farmer, handing over the money; "the liveryman wanted 15 shillings to drive inc home."

Could Mend It. A surly old farmer who had succeeded in quarrelling with everybody in the parish except the mild-mannered vicar, at length so tested that gentleman's temper as to force him to call the farmer a broken-down old mule. The farmer Sought the services of the village lawyer, who himself had suffered under the tongue of his would-be client. The man of law declared his powerlessness in such a case. "But he called , me a broken-down mule," yelled the farmer. . "Weil," said the lawyer, "don't come to me about that. I'm net a veterinary surgeon."

LORD AVEBURY'S SUCCESSOR.

The office of President of the National Association of Grocers' Assistants, which for the past thirteen years has been honourably occu- pied by Lord Avebury, was unhappily rendered vacant through his Lordship's death. Recently Mr. A.,H. Crosfield was approached by the officers of the Asso6iation and invited to fill the vacant\position, and, after an interview with a deputUtton, he has decided to accept this honourable position.

MAJORITY DOUBLED.

Wick's Emphatic Reply to Mr. Bonar Law.

Wick's reply to the Tory claim that the Gov- ernment has lost the confidence of the country is decisive and crushing. The Lord Advocate has not only retained the seat, but ,on a poll of just over 2,700 he has more than doubled his majority. The official figures are:—

Mr:R. Munro (L) Mr. A. G. Mackenzie (U)

_ Liberal majority As compared with the December election of 1910, these figures show an increase in the Liberal vote of 62, an increase in the Liberal majority of 232, and a decline in the Tory vote of 170.

After the declaration of the poll the Lord Ad- vocate addressed a cheering crowd from an hotel window.

Thanking the electors for their magnificent support, he recalled that Mr. Bonar Law had said: "Consult the people." Well (said Zfr. Munro), he had consulted the people of Wick Burghs, and he hoped Mr. Bonar Law liked the answer they had given him. (Cheers.) The election had been fought upon Home Rule first and Home Rule last, and was an emphatic en- dorsement of the Liberal position.

ECONOMY IN THE HOME.

At this time of the year the housewife is con- fronted with the problem of how to make her cakes sufficiently rich and tasty without undue expense. It is just at this time that Bird's Egg Substitute (Powder) becomes doubly valuable. Not only does it give that richness to cakes that is imparted by eggs, but it is much simpler in use, and there is no risk of tainted cakes. Cakes made with Bird's alternative for eggs are ex- ceptionally light, and it makes deliciously light Yorkshire puddings and pancakes. In cost, the comparison is overwhelmingly in favour of Bird's. A number of excellent recipes are given with each packet and tin, and a booklet contain- ing many more can be obtained post free by sending a postcard to Alfred Bird and Sons, Ltd., Birmingham.

AT LAST !—Continued.

House of Commons, be law, without being sen again to the Lords.

"For it never can be thought. by any man who has the blessing of reason, that there ought to be two authorities in a State, one capable of barring whatever the other would do. The powers of legislation exercised for the good of the people is good government; the powers of legislation exercised for the benefit of any set of men is bad government, and is naturally carried to excess; for the good of the set can only be pursued at the expense of the community. If we are told that this expedient of ours would no doubt be effectual to its end, if we could obtain it, but that to such a measure as this the Lords will never give their consent, we answer that, in a case of necessity, what cannot be obtained one way must in another; and the probability is that this being seen by the Lords, they will not hold out to the last.

"But if they do, the House of Commons have only to proceed a step, further, and declare that

Bills, as passed by them a certain number of them a certain number of times, and at certain intervals, are law. This resolution the people would hail with transport, and make the enact- ment laws by ' their obedience, and from that moment the House of Lords is blotted out."

The rest of this article is interesting, as show- ing that the House of Lords.was 78 years ago, as it is to-day, an unpatriotic and selfish mob. HENRY WHITHAM, 659, Broderick-street, San Fr4ncisco.

Long silence.

WONDERFUL LLOYD GEORGE.

To-day we publish an article by Mr. Henry Whitham, of San Francisco.

Years ago Mr. Whitham left Warrington, where he was engaged as a wire drawer at Mel;Vs. Rylands Brothers, Ltd.

He is a well-known working man writer on political and social subjects, and we welcoms his return to this kind of activity in the columns of "The Examiner."

Mr. Whitham's letter is notable for its appeal to an unknown public in his old town, after the lapse of many years.

There is also a joyful recognition of the feet that Mr. Lloyd George has taken up the crY of generations of old-time reformers, himself included, on behalf of the poor and suffering section of humanity, a cry which was uttered in the wilderness till the great-hearted Welsh" man gathered up the tangled skein, and the cause of the poor became, as by magic, pow tical politics.

But it is remarkable most of all for its quota- tion from a paper in pre-Victorian times, which not only ridiculed the farce of an Arre. sponsible House of Lords nullifying the work of the Commons„ but pointed out how the Lords' veto could be blotted out, the method suggested being practically that which Ms been so effectually adoptld by the present Liberal Government.

All things come to these who know how to waits is Mr. Whitham's philosophical reflection.

One who is, physically, far away from the old country, and yet keeps himself in prettY close touch with its doings, would like to have a few words with-who? I am almost afraid to venture an attempt to answer my own .question. Certainly a nelv generation has sprung up since October, 188'7. "Clifford Dix," previous to the above date, tried, and succeeded in at least one of his Con- tinued efforts, that was to cover' a fair amount of space in "The Examiner" with his stuff. At, that far-away time "Clifford Dix" found, what, the Henry Whitham of to-day finds, a paler' not only fit for the times and the home, but one that was proud to be neutral neither in politics nor yet in religion.

The Wrongs of the Poor. I received Copies of the London "Times" 011 also of the "Daily News and Leader," as sc -1 as possible, with full reports of the great, t..j wonderful, speeches of your own Lloyd George' I couldn't possibly tell you of the effect those speeches hat' on me. I was that excited I Or; tainly could not go on with my reading. would cry out., "Come here, .ma, and listen to this" :

The interests of agriculture have been sacri- ficed to other interests, whiCh are injurious to agriculture, and which to that extent are have' ful to the whole community. Now take the wages on the land. The general average °f wages paid on the land for its cultivation are lower than those paid in. any great industry is this country. (" Shame.") The hours of labour are longer than in any great industry. You have no Saturday half-holiday; you hay° many working on Sunday. It is not unskilled labour. (Cheers.) They talk about the work Of the labourer as if it were something anybodY could do who could just throw a spade over hie shoulder. . . . Twelve shillings a week, 13, 14, eked out with all sorts of allowances, has to keep the men from death and starvation. "Now what do you think of that?" ques- tioned my wife. - Why," she answered, "you have said that in 'The Examiner' a score of times." Yes, that is true, and hundreds of other men during the past fifty years have said the same thing. They were scorned and scoffed at, their names were simply by-words to those who plumed themselves on being "True-blue patri- ots "—those who mouthed the words "Suffer little Childreo to come lint° Me," yet never missed the chance to lake, not only the bread from the little one's .mouth, but the roof from Its head. "Grachus," "Nortliumbrian," "Davidson," "Verax," and a thousand more skilled writers have voiced the wrongs of the poor.

Warrington Field Club. A meeting was held in the Museum on Satur- day. There was a fair attendance of members, presided over by Mr. A. J. Jolley. There were also several visitors. Mr. Miln, of Garton's, Ltd., gave a very interesting lecture, illustrated by lantern slides, on "British Cereals." lie traced the history of the agriculture of the cereals, and exhibited excellent slides of the original members of the grass family, from . which man had produced our crops of to-clay., , The latter portion of his paper was confined to those members which were only suitable for these northern climes, via., wheat, oats, barley and rye. lie dealt very lucidly with the method of fertilisation and other botanical features of these grasses, and remarked it was accepted nowadays that they were all self-fertilised. Numerous slides were also shown of the types and the improvements made by the scientific plant breeder. A discussion was taken part in by several members and friends.

, Mayor and Municipal Employes. The Warrington and District Local Govern- ment Officers' Association held Os annual social evening and whist drive at the Town Hill on Friday evening. There were some 140 members .and friends present. His Worship the Mayor .tCouncillor Peacock) and Mayoress were pre- sent for the opening of the proceedings, but could not stay throughout the evening, owing to having accepted a previous invitation. Mr. J. Moore Murray, on behalf of the association, expressed the members' gratification and plea- sure at the kindness the Mayor and Mayoress had shown in joining them, even if it was only for a short while, and he wished them both a very prosperous and extended period of office. In replying, his Worship expressed the pleasure it gave the Mayoress and himself in being pre- sent, and he only regretted that, owing to a previous engagement, he could not stay very long. Ile thought that in Dr. Joseph (the Deputy Mayor) would be found an excellent , -'substitute for 'himself. The pleasure of the evening was greatly added to by the contribu- tion of violin solos by Miss A. Moar (leader of the first violins of Handley's Tutorial Orchestra, Bootle) and humorous items by Messrs. H. M. Ashton and J. H. Knowles. A very enjoyable evening ended by the distribution= of the whist •Prizes to the winning players by Dr. Joseph.

Life and Death.

Twenty female and seven male children were born in Warrington last week, giving a birth- rate of 19.6. Seven males and 17 females died, giving a rate of '16.8. Four deaths were due to cancer.

No Christmas Trams.

In response to the request of the tram em- ployee and the decision of the Council last week, the Tramways Committee have decided that no trams run on Christmas Day.

Tramway men and Sunday.

The Tramways Committee this week decided that on and after the first Sunday in the New Year the tram service for Sundays should start at 12.30 p.m., instead of soon after 9 a.m., as at present. This will enable the drivers and conductors to have a day off duty every alternate Sunday.

Territorials' Dance.

. The annual inspection dance in connection with the Territorials as held last night ,in the Drill Hall. There was a large attendance, and excellent music was supplied by the Territorial Band, under Bandmaster C. Wainwright. The 31.C.'s were Colour-Sergeant C. Walker, and Sergeant B. Appleton.

Christmas Decorations Doomed. A. small fire occurred yesterday afternoon at the sweet shop of Mrs. Elizabeth Wright, in Buttermarket-street. The window was showing the Christmas decorations, and was ignited whilst the gasses were being lighted. The Fire Brigade were called, but advised that.their ser- vices were not required before th'ey had a chance to leave the station.

For Deserted Mothers. The Warrington branch of the National Union of Women Suffrage Societies have arranged to make a collection at Market Gate, next Satur- day, December 20th, in aid of deserted mothers and widows of the town. Mr. Rowland, the secretary of the Guild of Hello, will superintend the distribution of the fund. Donations may be sent to Mrs. R. Pemberton, 9, Ellesmere-road, Stockton Heath.

Doctor Sacrifices Ills Life. Dr. Frank Woods died at Ramsgate on Wed- nesday morning -from blood poisoning contracted after an ,operation of a patient. He was the youngest Son of the late Mr. William Woods, of Warrington, and brother of Mr. Henry Woods, R.A. He was educated at Warrington. Gram- mar School, and then proceeded to Victoria University, Manchester, and St. Bartholomew's liospital,*. London.

Cycling Club Ball. There was a scene IA gaiety and festivity in the 'Parr Hall last night, when the 21st annual dance of the Warrington Cycling Club was held. Overlapping Tram Stages.

The Tramways Committee have decided to introduce, early in the New Year, intermediate or overlapping stations. One will enable pas- sengers to travel from Fletcher-street to Victoria-square, or Fletcher-street to Milner- street, passengers changing at Rylands-street, for id. each way. The penny fare on the Latch- ford /route will be from St. Mary's-street to Milner-street, or Fletcher-street; and on the Cemetery route from the Parochial Schools, Church-street, to the Jockey Bridge, Longford- street.

The Warrington Dock.

The Warrington Chamber of Commerce has I passed the following resolution: "That this Chamber hears with much regret that the Man- chester Ship Canal Company are proposing to fill in the old bed of the river at Wilderspool ; and that, as a suitable dock may eventually become essential to the commercial and indus- trial progress of the borough, and as this is the most suitable site available, the Chamber trusts that the Town Council will make every effort to retain the site for the purpose originally in- tended?'

Four Burning Accidents.

Curious enough, there have been four patients admitted into the Infirmary during the week, suffering from burns, but all, with the exception of one, are progressing favourably. Francis Percival (42), 94, Longford-street, was burned whilst employed by Messrs. J. Crosfield -and Sons. He was admitted on Thursday. Eliza- beth Burke (16), domestic servant, of Hall Cafe, Stockton Heath, was admitted on Monday; Mrs. Annie Lloyd (36), of 33, Hamilton-street, was admitted on Sunday, and Mary Jewitt (4), 11, Williamson's-square, whose condition is critical, was admitted yesterday, suffering from burns.

At the monthly meeting of the British Foundrymen's Association, held in the Manches- ter School of Technology on Saturday, a paper was read by Mr. W. H. The training of apprentices, vital as it was to the future of good craftsmanship, was never in a more un- satisfactory position, said the lecturer, than at the present time. Spiritualist Entertainment.

A concert, followed by a social, was held in the Druids' Hall on Tuesday evening, in con- nection with the local Spiritualist Society, and was well attended. Councillor Skeliand pre- sided, and stated that while he still carried on his work at the Ragged School, he felt perfectly justified in helping what he believed to be the good cause of Spiritualism. Songs were well sung by the Misses E. Tanner, M. Jones, and A. itoughton, Mr. Griffiths (humorous), and other items were a duet by Mrs. Speed and Mr. W. Gilbert, a humorous recitation by Mr. Gil- bert, and a capital sketch, entitled "Lord Tomkins," by members of the Buckley-street Sketch Party. Mrs. Speed and Mr. T. Tanner ably presided at the piano. The chairman and artistes were thanked, on the motion of the President, Mr. Stout, seconded by Mr. Foster; senior.

A Burst Driving Wheel. An inquest was held at NN, arrington on Thurs- day morning by Mr. S. Brighouse on Frederick Stockton, a single man, 23 years of age, who lived with his parents at 1, Hale-street, and who was killed by the bursting of a driving wheel on Monday morning at the Bewsey Forge of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles.—Mr. Thomas Llewellyn, foreman in charge of the engine, said that he passed through the " eight-inch " mill, where the accident occurred, a short while pre- vious to the explosion, and then the engine was running in a proper manner and was satisfac- tory.—James Holt, superintendent, stated that in May last the wheel was inspected, when it was discovered three arms were cracked. They had no wheels in stock at the time, and they repaired the wheel by strengthening it by the addition of plates, and that made it satisfactory. —The Coroner thought that the repairing seemed to have been done efficiently, and the jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death." An Unhappy Debut. Warrington's new motor fire engine made its debut to a local fire late on Sunday night, but it failed en route. The alarm was received at 11.15 that a fire had broken out at the St. Helens' Cable Works, and a full complement of men ' turned out on tlie motor engine. However, a stoppage was necessitated at the crossings near Messrs. Crosfield's works, and when they at- tempted, to proceed again it was discovered that something had gone wrong, for the engine re- fused to move. The assistance of an onlooker was obtained, and he righted the defect, only for the motor to again fail after travelling a few yards. No time was to be lost, and the members of the brigade rushed to the fire, a short distance away, and took appliances with them. In the meantime a few of the employes had been playing on the premises from the com- pany's hose, and, together with the aid of the Warrington Brigade men, all danger of the fire spreading to an adjoining building was avert?d. The horse engine " Major " was brought to the spot, but its services were not needed. The damage is estimated at ..Q2OO.

Human Locomotives.

At the Juvenile Court on Wednesday a num- ber of youths were charged with trespassing on the London and North-Western Railway, near the Black Bear Bridge. The police evidence showed that the defendants were pushing a large, eight-ton truck along the metals, and besides it being a danger to other people, the lads were risking their own lives.—They were each fined 2s. 6d. and costs. A Tale of Laziness. A pitiable tale of laziness was related at the Juvenile Court on Wednesday, when James H. Ashurst, of 64, Plumpton-street, was charged with neglecting to send his daughter Elizabeth to school. Mr. J. Moore Murray and two school attendance officers stated that it was a case of laziness, and one officer stated that often when he called at the house at mid-day the wife had to get up out of bed to answer the door.—A fine of 10s. and 6s. costs was imposed, with the alter- native of 14 days.

At the annual general meeting of the Emmanuel Tennis Club a report pf the past year's successful work was read, and a balance in hand was shown. The election of officers re- sulted in the treasurer and secretary being both re-elected, and the committee was chosen as follows: Rev. W. T. Jaye, Misses E. Jones and N. Gandy, and Mews. R. An old man of between 60 and 70 years of age died in the Workhouse on Sunday, and at an inquest on Thursday it was stated that one night last October he applied for lodging at a lodging-house, saying he had come from Aus- tralia and had no friends in England. He was admitted, and, on mounting the stairs, he fell and fractured his thigh. As he was unknown he was removed to the Workhouse, where he died from consumption. His name was believed to be John Walsh, but nothing could be found about him.—Verdict: "Death from natural causes."

Heavy Betting Penalty. George Ryder, living at 70, Chester-road, and carrying on business as a jobbing jeweller, was fined .125 and costs for using his shop in Bew- sey-street for betting purposes by the Warring- ton magistrates on Tuesday. The excuse was that Ryder had received "inside information" about horses, gave it to his friends, who got him to accept bets on behalf of a bookmaxer.— Joseph Bate, labourer, 16. Schoolrbrow, was fined £5 and costs for loitering in Bewsey-street for betting purposes. The police alleged that he met men and got bks. These he took to Ryder's shop.

In the opinion of the Rev. G. C. Briggs, the vicar of Christ Church, Latchford, sales of work are not necessary evils, as some would have them believe. In the first place, they were not necessary, and in the second they were not evils, but a useful and harmless way of "raising the wind." Women's Suffrage Debated.

A lively and interesting debate on the Suffrage question took place on Monday evening at the Junior Liberal Club in Horsemarket-street. Mr. E. Lloyd Jones took the affirmative and Mr. G. Toulmin the negative. Mr. J. 11. Booth occu- pied the chair and briefly introduced the sub- ject for debate. Mr. E. Lloyd Jones, in his opening address, said that woman demanded the suffrage because she was a subject and demanded political rights equal to those of men. Because suffrage would make a woman a power for good in politics, and would give her a direct interest in local and national questions, she would be able to give great assistance in making better and more just laws. "Women's wrongs," he continued, "will increase as econ- omic competition increases, and it is only right and necessary that she should have the vote for her own self-protection. Women have entered every profession, commercial and industrial, and demands equal rights with men. Women have trade unions, but no power, because they have not the vote. She is now a wage depend- ent, and demands the right to improve her conditions through the vote."—Mr. G. Toulmin said that it was an utter fallacy to say that women had any special wrongs that men were not willing and anxious to redress. He quoted Lord Loreburn, who stated that our laws are more merciful to women than to men. Women's trade unions have just as much protection from the State as men's, and were governed by exactly the same laws. The contention that women' should receive the same wages as men, he said, was a mistaken one. A woman worker was generally a single person, living in a family with other bread-winners. Women never entered a profession with the desire to spend her life there; she only looked upon it as a meant; whereby she could gain temporary independ- ence. If a man is a bachelor he generally has more calls upon his purse than an unmarried women, and had to look to the time when his wage and capital would have to be emplo-pd to find a home for himself and wife, and perhaps a family. And that is the time when the injustice of giving equal wages to men and , women is an injustice to hardest-worked and most useful women in the country. A woman who is married receives her wages through her husband, which can at the most be only a share of his wages, and if her husband only receives the same wage as a single woman it is a gross and monstrous injustice to the married woman. And the tendency of such conditions would be for women to prefer such a position to the more exacting duties of a household, to the detriment of the country as a whole.—Mr. W. Sanders, Mrs. Jones. Miss Booth and other ladies and gentle- men took part, and both speakers briefly re- sponded. On ,a vote being taken, the chair declared that "the No's' have it by two votes."

OLD MAN'S ORDEAL

Shot in a Wood, Terribly Wounded

and Crawls Home.

HIGH UGH SENSATION.

An extraordinary shooting affair is reported from High Legh. The victim of it is Joseph Skitt, 74 years, an ex-gamekeeper, of Hollowell Brow, and he was removed yesterday evening to the , Warrington Infirmary, suffering from severe injuries to his thigh, ascribed to a shot from what is known as a trap gun. An amazing story of the old man's pluck was elicited by "The Examiner" representative. Skitt was proceeding through a wood on the Knutsford side of High Legh, to meet a game- keeper friend, and in passing over some grass his foot caught a wire hidden in the grass. A second later there was an explosion from a hidden gun, and Skitt fell with a serious gun- shot wound in his thigh, from which he bled

profusely. Skitt cried loudly for help, but as the spot was a lonely one his pathetic cries were not heard, and realising the seriousness of his posi- tion he made an effort to get home. He stag- gered a short distance, and then fell into a ditch, from which he had the greatest difficulty in extricating himself.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
FOUND UNCONSCIOUS.

Getting out of the ditch, the old man literally crawled out of the wood homewards. Painfully he managed to reach the vicinity of his home, where he collapsed, and lay unconscious for some time, until he was found by two young men, who removed him into the house and summoned Dr. Thorpe, from Lymm. The doctor gave every assistance to the in- jured man, and ordered his removal to War- rington Infirmary, where he was taken in the ambulance van last evening. Skitt is suffering from severe loss of blood and shock.

-- -- A nephew, who was hurriedly summoned from Shropshire, seen by "The Examiner" repre- sentative, said it was a mystery how Skitt came by his injuries. As an old gamekeeper, he would possibly have been aware of the presence of trap guns in the wood, and would have acted accordingly. The guns were, it was alleged, placed there to keep poachers away, and evi- dently Skitt had forgotten about them when he went into the wood.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
NEAR A MAIN ARTERY.

"My uncle," he said, "is now progressing as favourably as can be expected, after his terrible experience. He has lost a large quan- tity of blood, and had the shot been half an inch nearer the inside of the leg it would have punctured the main artery, aloud he would pos- sibly have bled to death before he could have done anything to help himself." Mr. Skitt is one of the best known residents of High Legh, where he resides in. a cottage by himself. He is unmarried and is very popular in the district. Up to 20 years ago he was a gamekeeper, but .has since lived in retirement. The news of the extraordinary accident has created a profound sensation in High Legh and the district between Lymm and Knutsford. "FAIRLY SATISFACTORY." The report given at the Infirmary last even- ing to "The Examiner" representative was that " Skitt is injured severely. His condition is fairly satisfactory."

===== 0002601_19131213_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
INSURANCE CARDS.

Should Name of Trade Unions

Appear Upon Them?

THE MAYOR'S VIEW.

•.4 . The principal discussion at the monthly meeting of the Insurance Committee took place on the following resolution, proposed by Mr. Edes : "That we consider it highly important that the names of approved societies should ap- pear on all contribution cards, as this would be a great boon to the smooth working of the Act." Mr. Edes moved that the delegates, the Clerk, and the Chairman, Councillor Dr. Joseph, sub- mit that resolution to the Commissioners at a meeting the. next day. Giving instances of difficulties which existed, he said that people who travelled from town to town had no par- ticulars whatever. They had only a card, and nothing to show to which society they belonged. The societies could refuse to give them benefits. , The Chairman said he believed the Commis- sioners objected to such a proposal at the be- ginning, because it revealed the fact that a man was a trade unionist. The Mayor: That is so. Mr. Edes : The workers did not like it at the beginning, but it is not so now. Ido not think employers are such unreasonable beings to trouble whether a man is a trade unionist or not.

The Mayor's View. The Mayor: I hope this committee will have nothing to do with it. We don't want to put these men in the position of having to state their trade union on the card. Some employers do object to trade unionists. They object also to particular trade unions. Mr. Massey did not think it would be any benefit whatever to have the name on the card. The Clerk thought it would be unwise for them to do such a thing until public opinion had turned round in favour of the names being on the cards. At the Ant the Commissioners had thrashed the matter out. He suggested that the should have a distinguishing number. Councillor Roberts said he agreed with the Mayor to a certain extent. He could imagine it probably being unpleasant for an insured person in the time of a dispute with his em-

ployer. Mr. Edes agreed with the suggestion as to numbers. The Mayor: Keep the name of the cypher out,

lock, stock and barrel.. It was stated by a member that the trade unions would strongly object, but Councillor Roberts said he thought trade unions were well ' able to look after themselves. After further discussion the resolution in favour" of a mark was lost. 26,330 Insured. The Clerk said that the register on the 12th October, when the third count was taken, was as follows: Number of approved society mem- bers entitled to medical benefit, 26,330; deposit contributors, 408: as against 24,942 and 339 last year. The increase in deposit contributors was accounted for by the fact that a lot of them had only just been found. " Model" Employers.

The committee considered a recommendation of the Finance Committee to raise a junior clerk's salary to 16s. per week. It was explained that the boy was now getting 10s., and the opinion of several members was that he should get 18s. from the Ist of January. The Mayor said he was in favour of the wages of the staff being increased. The boy would get practically Bs. 6d. advance in 12 months, which example might with advan- tage be copied by other firms in the town. Dr. Joseph: The Mayor's suggestion is a good one. One or two members were of the opinion that the boy was getting enough, and one member said "lie is not the only clever boy in War- rington." It was decided that the Finance Committee consider the question again as to giving 18s. per wepk. versmi.

LAUDANUM TO SOOTHE ME BABY=

Warrington Young Mothers Said to be Deficient in Knowledge of Child Life.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
PLAIN TALK AT A LOCAL CONFERENCE.

The young children in Warrington are to receive extra care, and, if a new committee appointed for the purpose can make it pos- sible, Warrington will shoot ahead of any other town in the country in the matter of protecting child-life.

There %vat a lengthy discussion about the methods to be adopted at a conference held under the auspices of the Guild of Help, but every speaker agreed upon the urgent. neces- sity of undertaking the work.

Some painful stories illustrative of the clamouring need for such a scheme were narrated. The ignorance of mothers in the care of young children was stated to be almost incredulous.

A committee representative of the Council, Education Committee, and other active insti- tutions in the town, was appointed to go thoroughly into the matter, and to report at an early date to the Executive Committee of the Guild of Help.

The Warrington Town Hall on Tuesday night was the scene of one of the most important con- ferences ever held in the town. It related to the care of child life below school age, and was convened under the auspices of the Warrington Guild of Help. Mr. W. J. Rowland, the secre- tary, submitted a number of apologies. • The MAYOR, who presided, said the object of the Guild of Help must recommend itself to every good citizen, and to everyone who had the interest of the people at heart. At the last meeting of the Health Committee it was decided to appoint another nurse to look after the chil- dren between one and five yeas. (Hear, hear.) Thus Warrington had done something in the direction suggested by the Guild, and if the Guild could bring forward any further scheme to look after the children, or benefit the com- munity at large, he felt quite sure the Corpor- ation would welcome it readily.

of good habits was left undone; it meant that the child was exposed to the. hearing and seeing of much which he should rat hear or see. I was told that many of the children listen to the words of the street corner loafers, and when, at the age of five, they went to school they used oaths and other words that were shocking from anyone's lips, far less those of a child. I do not say they know what the words mean, but they use, when they get angry, the words they heard used by the angry men, the use of whose words passes naturally into the life of the children.

NO RAY OF HOPE.

The Rev. H. OGDEN said until they had a definite scheme there was no ray of hope. Canon Stevenson had referred to the street being the child's university, and to the child having to gain its English from the loafers at the street corners. He would ask in what part of War- rington would the child hear these oaths? He ventured to say that the loafers would not be found, nor the oaths heard, at the street corners \ of the better class of artisans. He could name numberless streets where no such loafers would gather, and he could name localities where they did congregate, and it was in those where they were up against the problem of overwhelming poverty. The problem wa's there, and there it must be met. _ _

Amongst the better-class working people there was a sensible knowledge of how to bring up a child, but in the poorer localities there was intense and most woeful ignorance. They would have to remove that, and if the meeting could find a scheme to do it there would be a practical outcome from that gathering.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
"DRAGGED UP BY MOTHERS."

Miss JANET BLAIR, of the Manchester Univer- sity Settlement in Ancoats, opened the confer- ence. She said there were many social evils at the root of the trouble. To have IA race of healthy men and women they must first raise the health •of the children. The problems of infant mortality were the problems of infant vitality. In poorer quarters, said Miss Blair, families grow quickly; the children seem more numer- ous, and the accommodation is more limited. I am even afraid the children are dragged up by mothers. They are not the victims of cruelty, but of too much kindness, and their life is made .qp of scoldings and caressings.. There is no time to reason with and to help in the mould- _

ing of the child's character in any way. We might make more use of our parks as children's nurseries, and I never can gee why instead of men attendants there we should not have a few women to look after children de- posited in the parks by tired and weary mothers for a few hours. It would be a great boon. It might be said by that we are undermining parental responsibility and independence amongst the poorer classes. Well, most of us were kept in nurseries, and we did not have our mothers all the time with us, and lam sure we did not think our mothers shirked their responsibilities, and we are, per- haps the most independent of people. We make a fuss if we have not got a cook or a housemaid, and therefore I think we ought to be careful how we speak abOut our poorer sisters. Miss Blair gave illustrations of the work done in Manchester day nurseries, where for 4d. or 6d. per week mothers could leave their children in comfort and safety.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
OUTWARD RESPECTABILITY.

Father TALLON said environment, physical defect and morality all came into the question. The environment question in Warrington was important. In his own district he could point out different spots, which if altered, would tend to elevate the people, if not in morality, in the out- ward respectability of parents and children. Out- ward respectability did not touch altogether the question of morality, and he thought they laid rather too much 'stress upon outward respecta- bility without touching the source and origin of moral defects seen in children. Life was sacred, because the source from which it came was sacred, namely, from God, and until they had brought that home to men and women they would fail to elevate and get a real moral tone in their citizens. Many people entered matri- mony with the idea of getting a home and hav- ing money to play with. They had no idea of the tremendous responsibility which came with parenthood. An effort should be made to induce mothers to realise their responsibility, not only to the State, but to God.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
CLASSES FOR MOTHERS.

The Rev. 11. OGDEN asked the number who attended the classes for mothers in Manchester. Miss TEALE, a superintendent of one of the schools, said the attendances differed in different neighbourhoods. ' Mr. OGDEN : What I am concerned about is the attendances from the poorest neighbour- hoods. From the most squalid portions of Man- chester is there a better attendance than from the better artisan quarters? Miss TEALE : The largest attendances are not from the very poorest districts, but in these districts the women come in largest number to the sewing classes. I agree that the very poor are not touched by the classes for mothers. Directly you get in contact with people with 18s. or 20s. a week you cannot touch them, and some other organisation is wanted to undertake this work.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
TAKEN YEARS TO LEARN.

Mr. IT. KINGDON, the chairman of the Medic& Inspection Sub-committee of the Education Committee, gave an instance of the great work being done in Bradford. In this country we were 20 years behind the Germans in the care of the children, and in the work being done we had no cause for congratulation. In Warrington the rate of infant mortality had been reduced to what was regarded as the wonderful figure of 92 per 1,000. Why was it wonderful? Because they recalled the time when it was over 200. But 92 was not a figure to boast about; it was a miserable thing. If they applied such a rate nationally it meant that, roughly, 100,000 of the babies born died every year. A significant fact was that among the middle class, who, without enjoying lux- uries, lived in comfortable surroundings, the infant mortality was 60 per cent. If they took it at 40 per cent. it meant that naturally 40,000 died every year. On the question of extending the school agr he thought that by allowing a child to leave 13 years that child left at the age when it con. be most impressed in every respect. The Go', ermnent would have to raise the school age, bm meanwhile the Education Committee could do it with by-laws. When they tried to do it, how- ever, they had bricks thrown at them. (Laugh- ter.) One councillor, who did not represent the Labour party, but was a candidate for a Labour constituency, said they were "education mad in Warrington." It was to the 'class that member represented they must look for help, because they were the people who were chiefly respon- sible for the present state of affairs, and had 'power to alter it. He hoped eventually to see all the voluntary agencies which were dealing with the question of child life grafted on to the main health machinery of the town, under the Medical Officer of Health.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
CHILDREN WHO SWEAR.

Canon MORLEY STEVENSON said they knew the extraordinary difficulties of mothers in some homes. It was all very well of them to talk as they did, but the fact was that in many homes a child at the age of three or four, when ho had his "piece" for breakfast, was sent out to play in the streets, and his life was largely a street-led life. ===== 0002601_19131213_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
FATHER'S BEER; MOTHER'S GIN.

Mr. B. S. TOWNEOE said they wanted to see a practical outcome of the conference. He had often talked to the poorer children in the Park, and learned that their meal had consisted of a little ice-cream or some pastry, and a sip of their father's beer or their mother's gin. (" Shame.") .These children afforded an ex- ample of the problem they had to face. The people who had the power to deal with it more than anyone else was the Education Committee. The children were in the melting pot, and the Education Committee could help enormously in arriving at a solution of the problem. The speaker then, to give the meeting some- thing definite, moved: "That a committee be appointed to consider whether aRy, and if so what, steps should he taken to improve the lot of children under school age, and this committee be requested to report to the Executive Committee of the Guild of Help."

===== 0002601_19131213_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
ECONOMIC BASIS.

The Rev. T. T. JABIES seconded, and said he agreed there was a deeper question than had yet been met by the experiments amongst the very poor, and that they would have to meet. There was the economic basis which livuld have to be dealt with as soon as possible. Some of them might not want to look that in the face, but it was there, and they must face it and see if it could not be dealt with effectively. They could not put people in a position where life and everything was uncertain and expect from them all the moral virtues. Alderman Ipits EVANS, in supporting, said the Health Committee were not a philanthropic society, but they were cordially in sympathy with those voluntary efforts which had been outlined that night, and which did not crime within their own immediate purview. Many of the bad conditions were due to abject poverty. He had known of mothers who had struggled until they lost heart, and if something Gould be done to brighten their lives it would be done. The resolution was carried.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NEW COMMITTEE.

Mr. F. W. MONKS moved that the committee be: The Mayor and Alderman James Evans, representing the Corporation; Mr. C. E. Parker and Mr. Holman Bingdon, representing the Education Committee• the Revs. Canon Steven- son, T. T. James and Tallon, Mr. B. S. Town- roe, Mrs. Broadbent, Mrs. Booth, Mrs. Dimelow, Mrs. Langdale, and Mr. W. J. Rowland. He said he had met many married women of 25 and 30 years who could neither read nor write. If they were so ignorant, it was God help the little ones' who were placed in their care.

Mr. C. E. PARKER seconded, and dealt with the question of infantile mortality. In regard to the deaths below one year, Warrington was lower than the 18 large towns in Lancashire, but in regard to the ages from four to five years it compared badly with the other towns. The committee was appointed as suggested.

LAUDANUM FOR BABY.

Miss M. BRoADBE!rr, speaking about the work done in connection with the Babies' Welcome, referred to the ignorance shown by Warrington mothers. "One mother," she said, actually brought some laudanum, because she considered it a good thing to soothe her baby." Mr. J. MOORE MURRAY, Director of Education, thought much of the trouble arose through girls being sent to work without domestic training. The little they had learnt at school was lost, and they were utterly unprepared to undertake the responsibilities of a home. He felt that little good would be done until it was recognised that the future mothers must have a better and a longer education. The Rev. Dr. MELLOR thought those in posi- tions of influence should not hesitate to spread knowledge on matters of sex among young people—they should address themselves freely and .unblushingly to those fundamental ques- tions! He appealed to the medical profession to give the mothers expert knowledge by means of tho proposed schools. A vote of thanks to the Mayor, proposed by Mr. ROWLAND, concluded the conference.

Labourists Enjoy Themselv es.—A very enjoy- able evening was spent at the Penketh Labour Club on Tuesday, when an. excellent concert was submitted. Mr. A. T. Clay presided, and the artistes were Misses M. Watson, Dodds, H. Hall, and Messrs. E. Walker, F. Hall, B. Smith, G. Walker, E. Sanders and the Prize Quartet Party. Tannery Band Honours.—The Penketh Tan- nery Brass Band did well in: a brass quartet contest held at Bolton on Saturday. The first part of the contest was open to all bands, and from the second nine of the leading prize bands were excluded. The competitors played their own selection. The awards of the adjudicator (Mr. Herbert Bennett, of Bolton) were as fol- lows :—First section: Penketh Tannery, War- rington (conductor, D. Dobson), 1; Wingates Temperance, 2; Skelmersdale. Old, 3; Good- shaw, 4. Second section : Skelmersdale Old, 1; Congleton Town, 2; •Earlestown, .Viaduct, 3; Penketh Tannery, 4.

DEATH OF MR. THOMAS GANDY.

A Popular and Esteemed Resident.

Quite a gloom has been cast over the village by the passing away, early on Saturday morn- ing, of one who was highly esteemed and re- spected in the district, in the person of Mr. Thomas Gandy, " Brierfield," Penketh. Mr. Gandy, who was 58 years of age, received his early education at the Friends' School, Pen- keth, and also at Grove Park, Wrexham. He ° then entered the furnishing -trade, passing from Messrs. Marsh, Jones and Cribb, of Leeds, to Messrs. Garnett & Son, of Chester, with whom he remained for many years. He afterwards joined a large furnishing firm in Cincinatti; where he gained a wide American experience. On his return to England, he took up a position in connection with the Progress Works, connected with the Penketh Tanning Co., a, position which failing health compelled him to 'relinquish. He , was a thorough sportsman, but Chiefly • with a gun. A kind-hearted, genial man, he was ever a friend of the poor, who haveindeed lost a good _ _ _ .

friend. He was eOnnected with the Methodist Church. He had a seizure, which proved fatal, about six weeks ago. He leaves a widow to mourn her loss.

STOCKTON HEATH

' TOWNSHIP TITTLE-TATTLE.

More light is wanted. Alexandra-road complains again. Yet there has been. brilliant moonlight During these past few nights. But the residents want gas—gas. They won't be happy until they Get more gas lamps. They may Possibly get them soon, for the Parochial Committee are considering. Stockton Heath firemen have been smiling. Warrington has given them the sniN. Stockton Heath tire lighters can always Get to a fire when it occurs. Warrington's new .motor could not do So. Congratulations.—At the meeting of Stockton Heath Parochial Committee on Tuesday, Conn- cillor`H. J. Westbrook was accorded a hearty welcome after his long and serious illness, and was congratulated upon his recovery.

Justices on the Bench.—The justices at the Police Court on Tuesday were Mr. W. Fletcher (in the chair), Captain Roberts, Mr. F. W. Monks, Mr. T. Baxter, and Mr. J. A. Ransome.

"Over the Hurdle."—William Burke, 15, Winwick-street, Warrington, was fined ss. and costs at Stockton Heath Police Court on Tues- day for stealing an iron hurdle, value is., at Whitley Superior, on December 2nd, the pro- perty of Benjamin James Parsonage, jun.—De- fendant de-flied the theft.

Workers' Houses.—At the conclusion of the Parochial Committee on Tuesday, Councillor C. Dukes asked it, was liecessa,ry .to give.anotice of motion if it would involve- expenditure of public funds, and on being answered in the negative he handed in the following motion: "That this Council is of opinion that owing to the scarcity of cottage property, the parish should seek powers to erect a number of work- men's dwellings."

Met " Friends" at Market.—Thomas Morris, farmer, Little Legh, was summoned at Stock- ton Heath Police Court on Tuesday, for having been drunk- in charge of a horse and was stated that on the 17th November defendant was in charge of a horse and trap in Victoria- square. It was seen that defendant had not proper control of the horse. Inspector Breese and an officer assisted him out of the trap.— Mr. Steel said Mr. Morris regretted his position. He was nearly 70 years of age, and there had been no previous conviction. He had been to the market on the day in question, and ;had met friends.—A fine of 10s. and costs was im- posed.

Let There be Light !—A. letter was read at the Parochial Committee meeting on Tuesday from a resident in Alexandra-road, complaining of insufficient lighting., The letter stated that. the Council received something like .e5O in rates from the district, and the only expenditure in- curred was in the emptying of ashpits and the provision of one street lamp.—Councillor Whit- ley said some time ago they had the matter up for discussion, and it was decided that they could not see their, way to erect a lamp. In his opinion it required more than one lamp, and there was no "doubt that owing to the unlevel state of the road it was dangerous to go aim-1g it when dark.—A member said two other resi- dents had written requesting the Council to erect lamps, and it was stated that one of these had a lamp near his house, whereupon the Chairman jocularly inquired, "Does he 'want this lamp inside or outside his house?—lt was eventually agreed that the matter be referred to the Lighting Committee.

A " Nugget" N ight.—A service of song enti- tled " The Californian Nugget" was submitted to a good audience at the Wesleyan Schoolroom on Monday, on the occasion of the season's third entertainment in connection with/ the Hill Cliff° and Stockton Heath Band of HOpe. Curtis eulogised the valu- able assistance that had been given by their friends and neighbours from Latchford, and proposed a. vote of. thanks. Mr. G. Whitfield seconded and expressed a hope that the Stockton' Heath Band of Nope would be able to return the compliment by giving an entertainment .at Latchford. The vote was carried unanimously.
