A AvEliarry PROBLEM.

The Bridge Topic Once More.

The time occupied in turning the Stockton Heath Swing Bridge was a topic raised at the Parochial Committee meeting on Tuesday Councillor Clarke said that for the last 20 years pedestrians had been allowed to cross tile bridge while it was settling into position after boats had passed through, but during the last few weeks that arrangement had been altered, and now all foot passengers and vehicles must wait until the bridge was in position. It took for that operation from a minute and a quarter to a minute and a half, and the consequence was that people had been kept longer than ever before. He had not heard that there had been any accident or complaint about the former practice. The new system was more. dangerous, because when the gates were thrown open, foot passengers, cyclists,, motor cars and horses and carts all streamed on to the bridge together.

Waste of Valuable Time. One day when he was at the bridge there were about 300 people on both sides, and if these people were kept a minute and a half it seemed to be a great waste' of valuable time. Ile thought they ought to send a lettei from that committee objecting to the alteration. Councillor Westbrook said he could not under- stand why the company had allowed foot passen- gers to go on the bridge before it was locked, and a cyclist, walking with his machine, refused: It was eventually decided to write as Coun- cillor Clarke suggested.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0190.txt | included=true | included =====
SANITATION QUESTION. •

parochial Committee's Scheme.

The question of converting the pail system in Stockton Heath to that of the water-carriage system was mentioned at the Parochial Com- mittee meeting in the Hall Cafe on Tuesday, when the Ulerk read the report of the Stockton Heath Sanitary Sub-committee's meeting, held on Noveipber 25th. . -

- This report stated that the sub-committee had considered the following Particulars: There are 813 houses On the Pail system, and taking the cost of conversion at .27 per house, this would entail an expenditure of £5,691. The cost of the repayment of a loan to this extent, spread over a period of 20 years and paid by half-yearly payments of principal and interest, would be £415 per year. Against this it could be esti- mated that the annual saving on the present contract for the removal of nightsoil by the emptying of pails would be about £225, and the saving of expenditure on new pails about £3O, making a total saving of £255. However, the additional cost to the parish would be ..2160 yearly; and it was stated that a penny rate would produce £7O.

The report sets forth that in the event of the owners 'agreeing to eontributn one-half of the cost of the conversions, the work would entail no additional burden upon the rates.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0191.txt | included=true | included =====
From a Health View.

The sub-committee resolved: " That from a sanitary and health point of view it is, in the opinion of this-subLcomMittee, advisable to con- vert the existing pail closets in the parish to the water carriage system, and as the properties weuld be thereby improved, it be a recom- mendation to the Parochial Committee that all owners be approached with a view to their con- tributing one-half the cost." . _

The sub-committee also considered the ques- tion of dealing with the removal of the refuie of the parish under the existing system, and it was resolved that it be a recommendation to the Parochial Committee that tenders for the work be at once advertised for.. In reply to a question, it was stated that the committee had power to compel the owners to convert the pail-closet to the ,water-carriage system, but the cost would be thrown on the rates.

'Councillor Whitley asked if the sewers were capable of taking any extra matter.

The Clerk: Yes

It was further stated that the drain connec- tions to the houses would be satisfactory for the new system. _ _ .

Councillor Pearson: I beg to propose that this report- be adopted. He understood that the next proceeding would be to get into communication with the owners, and if they declined to meet them in the way. suggested, they should proceed on their own lines. This was seconded and adopted.,,

RISLEY.

Independent Methodist Church, Fearnhead.— A social evening was held in the School on Sat- urday, when prizes were distributed to the fol- lowing for success in collecting on penny cards: Master E. Mason, obtaining the prize for most money collected; Mr. John Houghton and Master Fred Bold, receiving Bibles. Mr. Joshua Hughes occupied the chair. On Sunday, Open Sunday was observed, in the school, a large congregation being present. Mr. J. Barton occupied the chair and Mr. R. Bold gave an interesting address.

Well-known Teacher Retires.—Miss M. W. Wightman, who has retired under-the age limit from the ,teaching staff of the Risley. School, was presented by old scholars and friends with a travelling bag and dressing case combined. The Rev. J. J. Glover presided over a good gathering of subscribers to the testimonial fund, and Mr. Joseph Clare, an old scholar, handed over the gift to Miss Wightma,n. In doing so he dwelt on reminiscences of his school days, and hoped she would enjoy her well-earned rest. Miss Wight- man made a suitable reply, thanking the friends for their useful gift. Short addresses apprecia- tive of Miss Wightman's work as a teacher were given by Mr. Clarkson, Mr. Bouchier, Mr. Holden, and 'apologies for absence were. read from Mr. Wilson, H.M.1., of schools in the dis- trict, and Dr. Beckett, chairman of the local Education Committep. Mr. Jos. C.. Warburton, who had acted as treasurer to the fund, was thanked for his services.

The New Bridge.—The contract for the erec- tion of a bridge to carry the new road from Cherry-lar e across the Upper Lake to Crouch- ley-lane i. 3 been let to Mr. Harry Fairclough, who will commence the work immediately. Tile structure is to be of ferro-concrete, and will be similar in design to the new Warrington Bridge. It will have a span of 109 feet, with a rise of 24 feet.

-Drowning Mystery.--LDnring the week the body of Tito Alas Barker, 58 years of age, who lived at Agden 'Cottages, was recovered from the Bridgewater Canal near Burford Wharf. The deceased was an attendant on a threshing machine, and it is surmised that he was going alongside the canal on Thursday of last week, when he was seized by a violent coughing and fell during the coughing_ convulsions- into the water. He was married, but had no chil- dren. There was a doubt as to how Barker got into the water, and the jury decided to return an open verdict of `,` Found drowned."

Temperance Women.—That the committee of the Lynam branch of the British Women's Tem- perance Association is doing its best to main- tain the interest of the members and *public is evinced by the excellent speakers they get to address their meetings. On Tuesday evening a capital address was given. by Mrs. Winchester, of Liscard, the lion. treasurer of the Cheshire ,County Union. The speaker, in a graphic man- ner, gave an outline history of the Association from its inception just forty years ago, when the wdmen of Ohio commenced the "whisky war," down to the present time, showing its phenomenal growth throughout the civilised world and its beneficial influence on humanity, irrespective of race or colour. The World's Tribute of Interesting Items of News. QUAINT AND AinUSING.

The Mexicans have a turn for the picturesque which displays itself in. the street. names • of the capital. •There is "Love of God-street," "The Holy Ghost-street," "Sad Indian-street," "Street of the Wood Owls," -" Lost Child Street," and a cautionary "Pass If You Can-street." Shop names, too, in Mexico, nre•out of the common. A drug store -calls itself " Gate :of Heaven" .(Surely ambiguous !), and a drinking saloon :describes itself, frankly. as "The Bait of • the 'Devil " I .

Evil of Stuttering.

Though much stuttering is due to such cases as heredity, imitation and fear, Dr. F. A. Bryant states that in many cases no explanation can be given. It is usually begun before the ace of seven, is known in persons of eighty, and, be- sides producing nervous disorders, prevents full aeration of the lungs, and thus gives rise to lung diseases and to morbid conditions from unoxygepated blood. In an industrial school, the breathing capacity of nine stuttering boys of 12 and 13 averaged 88 and 109 cubic inches, that of non-stutterers of same age averaging 166 cubic inches.

Trips to the Moon.

M. Esnault Pelterie, one of the ,best-known French aviatiori experts, is of opinion that the day will come when communication between the earth and the moon and stars will be pos- sible in aeroplanes. Providing that radium is one 'day found .in larger ,quantities, .and that some way! of harnessing its energies is discov- ered.by Scientiats; he calculates that an aero- plane weighing a ton should be able to cover the 238,000 miles which separate the earth and moon in three hours and five minutes. About 8001b.' of radium would be necessary to provide the fuel for the journey. At the current price about two thousand million pounds' worth would be required.

, Eat Cheese for - Meat. A physician claim that cheese is .a most. im- portant element in the food of workmen. For centuries- Englishmen have been great beef eaters, and as a ConSetiuence their systems have developed a great need for meaty (proteid) foods. Meat is becoming higher in price every day, and the poor man finds himself with the meat appe- tite, but not a meat pocket-book. The only way he can save himself is by taking to cheese.. It is claimed that there is as large an amount of proteid in a half-pound of cheese as there is in a pound of meat, and it costs, taking the rate of consumption into account, we suppose,, less than one-fourth as much.—" Science Siftings."

Red Hair.

"All red-haired women dr(jealous," accord- ing to Professor C. 13. DavenpM.t, of the Univer- sity of Missouri. "When .a red-haired woman has dark blue or violet eyes and dark eye- lashes, she is good to have • for a friend and bad to have for an enemy, is 'catty.' A curious antipathy is that of red-haired persons of oppo- site sex for each other. Among thousands of matings I have found only two cases where both husband and`wife are red-headed, and I am assured by red-haired persons that the anti- pathy exists. If, as is. sometimes alleged, red hair is frequently associated with a condition of nervous irritability, this is a eugenic anti- pathy."

The "Language" of Monkeys. Concerning the supposed languag6 of anthro- poid apes, Professor Boutan has recently taken up the question, and arrives at a negative result.: It will be remembered that Dr. Gamier came to the conclusion that monkeys have • a language analogous to human language, and express themselves by signs ..as well as by sounds which they emit.- According to Pro- fessor Boutan, there is only a difference or degree between this and human spee'ch and not one of kind. He observed the sounds made by a gibbon which he had captured When young, and his experiments .cover more thdn (lye years. He finds that the animal can pro- duce- only spontaneous and instinctive sounds corresponding' to a .state of satisfaction, or the like, or again hi -it Slate Of uneasines'Slir fear; also great excitement, and the sounds do not .appear to correspond to a real language, hut rather to what he - calls ." pseudo-language." While sounds of real language are acquired by education, those of s' ado-language are purely spontaneous. and fie thus differs froill Dr. Garnier's .conclu6iOns,

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

button School Question. The reference to "outside children " in the course of the discussion of this question at the 'Runcorn Guardians' meeting reminded me of a school lock-out that once occurred in i dis- trict in which I worked for some years. When I first went to the place there was an extensive School 'Board area, embracing two towns and several villages. One of the former obtained a Charter of Incorporation, and proceeded to form its own borough School Board. On the easterly border was a scho.ol to which a num- ber of children from beyond had been accus- tomed to &, because it was the nearest to their scattered homes in, the rural area. The Borough Board declined further responsibility on account of these unless the County Author- ities assured them of compensation. The latter declined to make any grant, whereupon it was resolved to exclude the' youngsters in question. Then, of course, the parents began to agitate, for the only available school in the opposite direction was far, far away. The ultimate result, after a prolonged display of: obstinacy, was an arrangement whereby the locked-out section were re-admitted, the County Education Committee paying amply to ensure continuance of the old conditions.,

===== 0002601_19131213_art0206.txt | included=true | included =====
Greece.

The incidental allusions to this ,country in last week's "Women's Questions" Column and in "Table Talk" somehow recalled a little experience which a Methodist, minister told me of the other. week. He said that whenever he conducted .a prayer meeting at a certain chapel in his circuit one of the leaders invariably prayed for "showers of grease." Of course, he was himself profoundly thankful that that particular petition was never answered. The same reverend gentleman narrates a story of a voyager who was impressed by the whiteness Of land which was sighted in the distance. So he went to the captain And begged him to explain the peculiarity. "That, sir," was the reply, "mis Greece." Is it., indeed?" said the passenger ; well, now, I thought it was snow !" Ghostly Pranks.

It. would be easy to support the claim set. up in the paragraph heaaed "Ghostly Jokes" in last week's "Literary Gleanings." There was the one that played pranks upon the residents at Asfordsby Rectory, Leicestershire, for over 30 years, and that only a few months ago was laid low by the rector, who, donning cassock. and -surplice, solemnly exorcised it.,s During the present year, too, Strabane, Ireland, has had its ghost, the head constable taking down the statements of. some railwaymen who re- ported • having seen the apparition. John Pinkerton, an esngine cleaner, describing the lirst visit of the "ghost," said that it appeared in the cleaning-shed after knocking loudly at the door. It gave unearthly yells. Armed with a crowbar, a signalman made a dash at the spirit, but could not strike it down. On the second night seven men saw the "ghost." It appeared again on a subsequent night, and "disappeared through a window.' "The form was that of a man of stout build," stated an- other engine cleaner. "It ran under, the engine, in front of it, behind it, and every- where.. We shivered."-

Bewitched Maidservant. While I was in Ilalifax I had to record many more or less mysterious happenings, and I may lust mention one concerning a domestic, servant who seemed' to me to, be rather a handy sort of person to have-around,,speCially during a strike of colliers. This is how the fun began. The occupants of the house, who at the time referred to had this treasure in an earthern vessel, heard a tapping at one of their windows on the ground floor, and, on .the , cause being searched' for, a considerable quan- tity of coal was observed to be falling. To some extent the coal appeared to fall from the sky, but good deal seemed to come from the roof of one of the houses. An investigation of -site roof, however; failed to reveal' any Clue to the source. Toward midnight the shower teased, but instead of the coals falling from the roof they began to fall inside the houSe, and all Monday and Tuesday, at irregular .periods, coals fell in various rrooms. Sometimes pieces of coal fell singly, and sometimes a large num- ber of pieces would come together as though someone were *emptying the contents of a scuttle thFough a hole in the ceiling. ,It was noticed that a great quantity came down the stairs, and, when, the door at the foot of the stairs was closed, it was bombarded with frag- ments which struck against it with sufficient force to make permanent marks on the wood-- work. It was observed that the infeerious showers only ocairred when the servant maid —who had only Jut been engaged—was in the house, and, on being questioned, she confessed that similar occurrences had taken place at her home, where not only coal but hatpins, hair-, pins, buttons, and the like often fell apparently from nowhere. The -bewitched maidservf,nt soon left, and after her -departure the, extra- ordinary showers ceased. I interviewed neigh- bours and other independent and disinterested witnesses, and their assured me of the literal accuracy of the haunted householders' version. I could fill my column with similar experience's, but, ,this being the special month for such stories, I will not further anticipate what other " Examiner " contributors may have to write on the seasonable theme.

"Nay, 'twor a -bad, do. ,Ah'd put it pockit, an' w'en:.they comes ter weigh in ah,eudna' finn'd it .onyweer. But at neet w'en ah gets 'oam ah fun it i' me watch key !" The Olfactory Nerve.

Two men with whom I have worked on dif- ferent-- papers have lost the sense of smell entirely. I imagine that if this should catch the eye of Councillor Gough, who, so bitterly complained to the ,Health Comthittee of the Warrington Town, Council respecting. offensive smells from works near his home, he will be ready to turn oily& green with envy. I have 'a friend who last season went on holiday to Ostend, and left that • resort prematurely be- cause he was unable to endure the bad smells Which he encountered there of all places! Many years ago, Dr. Bowman, the • eminent Chemist, facetiously declared in ,my hearing that in the corridor of one hotel in Germany he detected no fewer than seven separate and dis- tinct odours of an 'objectionable kind. After all, there are much worse places than War- lington for evil scents.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 13, 1913.

ME CREATION of TsE wopLD.

The Clay Books of Ancient Babylon and the

Remarkable Records They Contain.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0209.txt | included=true | included =====
THE STORY OF THE DAYS OF CREATION, THE DELUGE AND THE ARK.

Among the very interesting things which the explorers of Babylon are bringing to the sur- face there is nothing that has more human interest than the-nuNerous books from the old libraries. Big palaces and towers are, of course, very valuable as revealing the skill of the ancient peoples, but their books show us what they read and believed, with what stories they amused their children, and give us also their superstitions and fears and quaint beliefs. Several very long chapters could be prepared .upon the contents of Babylonish and 'Assyrian books, and there would be much to amuse us in the ancient riddles and children's reading books, to say nothing of the charms and in- cantations used by older folks to prevent danger and disease. But we are selecting for the pre- sent some records which everybody, perhaps,

the Tower of Babel and the scattering of man- kind. The legend says that men had "turned against the father of all the gods" -under a leader who was evil. At Babylon they began to build a mound and a tower, but the Winds destroyed it in a night, and the god .Anu "con- founded great and small on the mound. and confounded their speech and made strange their counsel." The word for confounded in this passage is the eNact word used in the -Book of Genesis.

It is rather curious to notice that Xisuthos in the Deluge story not only resembles Noah, but Enoch also, for it is said that he was carried away by the gods and did not die like any of the rest of mankind: We have also in the narrative the three sons Of Noah-. .Shem is called on the tablets Sarhu,

===== 0002601_19131213_art0210.txt | included=true | included =====
SCHOOLDAYS.

"Dad's going to give me a watch," Willie informed his aunt.

"That will be nice," replied his aunt, "and it will help you getting to school in the morn- ing."

"Yes, that's so, 'cause when I get to school I can .look at the watch_and see just how late I am."

THE NEW BABY.

"I hear you have a new baby at your house," said the grocer to. Susie. "Is it a girl or a boy?"

"It's a boy," said Susie, "and I wish it was a girl." _ _

"Maybe you could get it exchanged," sug- gested the grocer. "I'm afraid not,".. said the little girl sadly, "you see we have used it four days."

===== 0002601_19131213_art0211.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HIDDEN PEARL.

Deep down in the sea lived and grew a pearl in an oyster-shell. It grew and grew until it was the size of a tiny pear, and was all the colours of the rainbow. It took exactly seven- teen years to grow. At the end of that time a fairy found it and presented it to the queen. the queen was quite young and very beautiful, also as-!,good as she was fair. ..She ruled her kingdom wisely and Well. Her name was Rosamond. Although her subjecfs loved her, they desired a king, but no one from far and near was good enough for Queen Rosamond. - Now, all round the borders' of the -kingdom were high towers, guarded by soldiers, who were supposed to be constantly on the look-out for the enemy. But the queen didn't seem to have any enemies; and her faithful subjects loved home and peace too well to desire fame ,as warriors. So, when townsfolks, were busy over trade, and country folk going to market, and•the soldiers enjoying themselves and- mak- ing merry instead of keeping watch, the enemy came to the kingdom and invaded it. The hostile king commanded that no-soldiers should enter the queen's palace or the gardens. King Elwin was a warrior, handsome And bold, , but as no battle could. be fought, since the people were all prisoners, he went to the queen , and said he would be quite willing to withdraw his troops and restore - the kingdom to Queen Rosamond again if she would make ,him a present of a beautiful- Pear-shaped pearl he heard she wore on a gold necklace, the like of which had never been Seen before or since. In her anxiety for the safety of her subjects, the queen granted the request, but, strange to ' say, the pearl had disappeared! One night, when the people were all asleep, the king wept to the palace, stole the pearl, and hid it in an old oak tree in the palaCe garden, the inside of which was quite hollow: The king climbed up the tree, and, fastening 'a rope to one of the branches, easily let hirnself down inside. He next-took the casket which contained the pearl, and buried it down very: deep, afterwards cover- ing up the place us' it was before. He slipped back just before daylight, and nobody knew 'anything about it. For months the kingdom was searched high and low for the Missing pearl, but nobody could find it, and none thought of looking in the oak. At last the queen issued 'd proclamation that whoever, could lind the pearl should have her hand in marriage. Now, this was just what King Elwin had been waiting for, lie h.ad fallen in love with Queen Rosamond the first time he saw her,,, and while the people were huriting for the lost pearl, had amused himself by observing the cUstoins of the people and the rich agricultural country, which he confessed to himself was better than his own ; the people, too, were More industrious., and he thought how mwil better it would be for them if they had :a king.

One day, when Queen Rosamond was sadly walking about the palace gardens thinking' of her poor subjects, King Elwin approached.,her and asked her hand in marriage, as he knew where the pearl was. Now the queen was very haughty, the more so to King Elwin, as she con- sidered him the , cause of all her misfortune. Quietly she told him, that he who married her must first bring the pearl to her and restore the kingdom to its former state. At this the Icing. joyfully presented her with the casket, and said he would do anything to please her if she married him. Queen Rosamond consented, and soon the glad news spread far and near, and enemies became friends.

At the marriage ,feast King Elwin was asked how he had found the lost pearl, but this he hesitated to confess, for he now saw he had done wrong. But he could not avoid it, and the story had to be told. Afterwards. he begged forgiveness, first from the queen, and after- wards from the people for deceiving them. and laid that it was his great love for Queen Rosa- mond pat had led him to do it. But he. would never take it from her again, which put every- one in a good humour. _ _

The queen replied that she was bound to for- give one who had confessed so honourably, and her subjects saia they were delighted to have a king. So all were restored once more to peace and happiness, harmony and love.

CAPTAIN DROWNED AT WIDNES.

George Gandy (42), a ketch captain, was re- covered from the Widnes Old Dock on Sunday evening. At the inquest the evidence showed that Gandy was seen. late on Saturday night in h perfectly sober condition. Next day his cap was found floating on the water Rehr his boat, and the body was recovered at the same spot. A ladder used for boarding the ketch was in its position against the wall of the dock.- . It was stated that Gandy was shortsighted, hnd.Coroner Brighouse remarked that Saturday night‘was very dark and stormy, and it looked as though Gandy had slipped into the water when proceeding down the ladder.—The verdict was "Accidental death."

THE WORLD OF LETTERS.

*PROPERTY,

Authoritative Protest Against an Over-dominant Individualism.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0214.txt | included=true | included =====
DUTIES AND RIGHTS.

A book of considerable value, combining as it does the. essays of different writers on various phases of the question of the ownership of pro- perty, has just been opportunely published by Messrs. Macmillan and Co., Ltd. The essays, which, collectively, go to the root of the land question, constitute an historical; philosophical, and religious survey of the problems which gather round the holding of property. There are seven essays, and. although they differ in emphasis on particular points, there is a general agreement running through the whole. The whOle, work is a protest against an over- dominant individualism, the assertion of the unqualified right Of acquiring, retaining, and perpetrating property Alien such creates con- ditions which render it almost impossible for many of the citizens of a State to develop their manhood and womanhood. There is one point which .receives prominence- in the essay of H. G. Wood, of Birmingham, which is fresh to us. We refer to the influence of the Reformation on ideas concerning wealth and :property. The writer traces the growth of individualism to the sense of individual respon- sibility, the outcome of the Reformation: This at first was especially in religious things.. The -Reformation emphaSized the idea of each man standing in his own lot, each man being respon- sible to and having free and 'independent right of access to his Creator. This separate indivi- dual right 'naturally extended-itself to material things; it removed, amongst other things, the moral witness of the Church (contaminated as it may have become) in regard to its moral witness .as to the use of wealth. But it failed .to, supply anything adequate in its place. Puritanism removed restrictions in the acquisi- tion-of .Money. lt, developed a public conscience which insisted on labour and sobriety for the poor, but.made no attempt to check. the accu- mulation of wealth. -True, it did concern, itself about usury, and a law was passed prohibiting a lender to exact more. interest than 6 per cent. But, on the whole, its chief emphasis was upon the freedom of the individual to do what he would with his own as long as he 'observed the laws, human and divine. It would be unfair, to blame the Puritans, because they failed to anticipate the social evils which, to some extent, .spring out of this very emphasis on individualism. No doubt one di the fruits of this can be found in the growth of what is generally known- as .the Manchester School of Politics, and the principle of Laissez Faire, the rooted trust in individual responsibility and self-help. Honesty in bargaining was part, of their religious inheritance. The only fault that can be found with this product of the Reformation, if such it be, is. not that they were wrong in their particular tenets, but that they failed to realise that these tenets did not embody the whole of the required truths. . .

Mr. L. T. Hobhouse contributes a brilliant essay on the "Historical Evolution of Property," in which he differentiates between " property for use' and ,"property for power."- He illus- trates the difference between control and use of things which give • freedom and security, and the control of persons through things which give power to the owner.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0215.txt | included=true | included =====
DIVINE RIGHT OF EVERY MAN.

Possibly the best way to give an idea of the purpose and niain thought of the book is to cite a number of the pregnant passages in which the book abounds. .

We will .with the 'introduction by Bishop Gore. lie says: • •

We are bound to believe that, 'whatever in- equalities must subsist among men, every man has the divine and equal right to realise him- self. The success of a:civilisation for 'us must be measured not by the amount and character of its products or material wealth, nor by the degree of well-being which it renders possible for a privileged class, but by the degree in which it enables all its members to feel that an adequate measure of free self-realisation is granted them. On this ground then our civili- sation is open to the most serious indict- ment

A state must be judged, and should judge itself, by its tendency to generate in all its citizens. a worthy type of life. If at any stage it finds that the institution of property, as it exists, is fostering luxury and exaggerated power in a few, and enslaving or hindering the many, there is nothing to prevent it rectifying whIA is amiss. Property is relative to char- acter: it is a means towards a good life,, and a good life for all men.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0216.txt | included=true | included =====
PROPERTY AND PERSONALITY.

The Rev. H. Scott Holland, who writes on property and. personality, refers to the teachings of the Bible and the early Fathers, and says The result of such a tradition might be summed up in the historic phrase that all pro- perty was held in trust. Those who used if had to answer to God, as good stewards, for its use. There was no' moral or legal possibility of standing on the bare claim of possession. The possession of private property was conditional, not absolute. And its public utility mist be -justified, and answered for„at the bar of diVine judgment. The masses of workers have dropped to the position of pure wage-earners. Their exist- ence is never in their 'own hands; nor are they responsible for their own maintenance. The stability, the power to look beforQ. and after, the assured hold on reality, the embodiment of their own wills in a material fact, which we philosophically recognise to be the Moral and spiritual value of private ownership—all this is denied them.

Present conditions least the vast multitude of workers to become mere items on the surface, without any secured future, without any sure grip on facts,. without any stored reserve, with- out any established status. Personal value finds no public Witness. Character has no firm pivot round which it can build up its fabric. The inner life misses its outer support. We must establish the upremacy of justice over all conditions under which private pro- p2rty is held.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
Property Held in Common.

Men may win the moral qualities which the sense of property evokes, by owning things in

common.

• Every individual citizen, who is conscious of what his citizenship means, gains,. so •far, the ethical value Of a collective owner. He is moralised by such self-possession, • mediated for him through his membership in the State. Collective ownership may come into play over and beyond the area which private ownership can'cover, and so can spread out, for the many, the opportunity which our present system con- fines to the few. And this will depend on how far collective ownership can work upon the individual conscience and imagination with the same force as we now attribute to private pro- prietorship. The confidence with which we meet this last inquiry will depend entirely on our psychological estimate of personality. . The writer does not carry his inquiry further as to the psychological estimate Of personality. He simply indicates, as above, that collective ownership may to some extent compensate for individual ownership.

Back to God all rights run. His righteousness is the bond of all human fellowship. Man's authority to say of anything "that is mine-" restc, finally, on his power to say "I am God's."

Literary Gleanings.

THE SPARROW OF D

Writing in an essay after a visit to Italy, Waterton makes some -interesting speculations as to the species of bird referred : to in the Psalms of David as a sparrow, and says: '' The Royal Psalmist, whilst bending down in peni- tential prayer *fore his offended Maker,' ex- claims: ' I have watched and am becom as a sparrow all alone on the house-top.' I have often wondered .what bird this could be, know- ing, by daily experience, that, it could not. be the house sparrOws for the house-sparrow is not solitary in its habits. My arrival in Rome let me at once into the secret. The • bird to which the repentant King of Israel compared himself is a real thrush in size, shape, -habits, and in song, with this difference from the rest of the tribe, that it is remarkable throughout all the East for sitting solitary-on the •hablfa- tions of man. I found out the bird was the. true solitary thrush', ' and paid particular at- tention to its habits. It only associates With its own mate in .breeding time; and even then it is often seen quite alone upon the house-tops, where it warbles its sweet and plaintive strains, and continues its song as it moves in easy flight from roof to roof. It breeds in holes in the walls, and lays five eggs of a very pale blue. They .much resemble those of our star- ling. The bird itself is blue, with blaCk wings and tail. The solitary thrush is seen in all countries of the East up to Syria and Egypt."

===== 0002601_19131213_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
TIIE BOOkLOVER'S LONDON.

The man who is tired of reading about Lon- don is no true Londoner. His heart.warms to every recollection of the streets that he knew as a boy, most of which have changed, god some of whfch are marvellously preserved. In Mr. St. John Adcock's London, the London of Defoe and Fielding, of Ainsworth, Dickens, and Thackeray, and of Besant and Gissing, the two authentic Londoners of the ,last generation of novelists—the old places and streets and houses never fade. They are there for ever, and splen- did people walk in them. You. can meet David .Copperfield at every turn; and Esmond is there, bowing familiarly to .Henrietta Temple; though she was not of his day; and Evelina ; and Nigel, pursuing his fortunes not far from Fleet-street. Nay, while in the neighbourhood of Staple Inn you may even question Edwin Drood about his mystery; and going along Holborn you would at last come to the guests of the famous dinner party which Gilead P. Beck gave in "The Golden Butterfly." But London is peopled with great shadows; it is full of the great 'houses of. the past, and, there is hardly a yard of its chattering streets that cannot be made inter- esting by those who know' it well. Such a guide is Mr. -St. John Adcock with whom one may never have a dull moment.

RUSSIAN PILGRIMS TO JERUSALEM.

In England and in Western -Europe gener- ally this is not the age of simple faith, but Mr. Stephen Graham's unique books reminds us that that age has not everywhere passed, away. His narrative brings us into an atmosphere most strangely compounded of mediEevalism and modernity, but in all essentials the Russian pilgrim appears as far more suggestive of the primitive era of Christianity than of the twen- tieth century. It is true- that much of the long journey may now be travelled by train, that the last stage' is a sea voyage, and that the Christian pilgrim is no. longer liable tb be murdered on hostile territory by Moslem fdeS. But before embark- ing at Odessa many of the peasant devotees have traversed enormous 'distances on foot. and they still suffer with the utmost cheerfulness hardships which to the people of almost any other European nation would seem intolerable,. author's'account of the passage in the pil- grims' :steamboats from Constantinople to Jaffa would in some respects be revolting were it not that the picture of herded squalor and extreme physical discomfort throws into higher relief the spiritual fineness of the men and women who endure these things with serenity of soul. Most of them are aged folk, and the pilgrimage is the crowning act of their lives. Many die before it is accomplished. The un- wonted exertions, long marches, privations, fastings, and even the sheer pressure of the crowds that gather at the holy places in Jerusa- lem prove. too much for worhFout frames. And yet it is in no spiplt of fakir-like asceticism that the moujik enter S on his holy and hazardous quest. "The pilgrim's -ideal," says the Rus- sian authority whom Mr. Graham quotes, "is a sweet feeling of the heart in prayer."

. Mr. Graham thus describes his first view of the Sepulchre:. "It was a surprise. The whole was so ruined, so patched and grimed, so ancient, and withal so enigmatical. It seemed as if it might have been produced_ Only the night before by Some evil magician. Certainly that round which the, Crusader: and the Saracen had fought-, and round. which the Arab hawkers loafed and screamed, was not beautiful. It had in it an appearance of death.

This is really rather a horror to the fastidious. The noise" about it and the offal of the East are appalling. What shall one say of the Turkish gendarme sprawling on a sofa at the entrance smoking his cigarette and lazily looking at his half-drunk cup of coffee? Even within there is heard the noise of the incautious movements of'Greek and Armenian priests; the church is vast and strange, ruined, dirty beyond won is, With VerminouS walls all cracked and chipped. One has entered into a mysterious and awful , chamber."

BABYLONISH BOOKS.

We must, first of all, understand what ancient books were likes They, may have had parch- mentS; of this we are not quite sure. They certainly .had papyrus to write upon. But the common, every-day' book was made of clay, and many thousands of them have been dug up and carried to the Mu'seums of London, Berlin, Paris and America, to be deciphered by Western scholars. They toOk a small slab of clay, wrote upon it with a tool, and. then allowed it to dry in the sun. The people of Babylon were a literary people and filled large libraries with these clay books, but, as might have been ex- pected, after so long a period of time and the destruction of the cities by their enemies, the hooks are found only in fragments. - Yet we have a very accurate idea of .the con- tents of the books of ancient Babylon. Happily for us, when the Assyrians gained power they established a hUge library at Nineveh. Assur- bani-pal, one of the Assyrian kings, plundered 'the library of Babylon and— Set an army of scribes at work to copy the writings. The king tells us very often that the gods "had given him broad ears and enlightened his eyes" to see the-value of the old records at Babylon. We or very glad for those broad cars and enlightened eyes, because the copies he caused to .be made have been very well preserved under the desert soil, and we can read admiraole copies of books which were written 2,000 years before the Christian era.

The Assyritui books were raper different from the Liabylonish 'Ones, They baked them _in oi,enB, and we can see the holes made in them by which the moisture' escaped. And as Bmall tablets were less likely to crack in the ovens than large ones, they made them of very small size. _

The important thing is that these clay tablets made about 700 years B.C. are exact copies of tablet which Were written 2000 to 2500 8.C., and which contain the wisdom, beliefs, and history of an age which was almost forgotten. And among them are fragments of Several accounts of the Creation and Deluge. TIIE ACCOUNT OF :THE CREATION. - •

It is just about forty-two years since the first of these was found by Mr. George Smith. It was a .mere fragment, but it raised quite a flutter of excitement and interest. Here is a translation :

At that time the heavens above named not

a name, Nor did the earth below record one : Yea, the deep was their first creator, The flood of the sea was she who bore them all. Their waters were gathered in one place and The clouds (?) were not collected, the plants were not grown. At that time the gods had not issued forth, any one of them. By no name were they recorded Then the great gods were made. Lakhmn and Lakhamn issued forth. They grew up ... Next were made the host of heaven and earth, The time was long then . The gods Anu .. • • The host of heaven and earth.

The account_breaks off here, but another tab- let, the fifth of a series, tells that someone "made the stations of the great gods, even the stars, fixing the places of the principal stars. . . He ordered the year,'setting over it the decans ; yea he established three stars for each .of the twelve months." , Another fragment says: "At that time the gods in their assembly created (living creatures). They made beautiful the mighty . . . . They made living beings to come forth, the cattle of the .field, the beasts of the field, and the creeping thing." What follows cannbt ,be deciphered.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0225.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DELUGE STORY.

_ The story of the Deluge ,as they told it, ap- pears ,in ever so '.many fragments - of clay writing. So numerous are they that by, com- paring them we can get a fairly complete story. The hero is Xisuthos, and he is told by the gods to build a ship, because the earth is, to be destroyed by water. Into the ship he takes his family, 'cattle, beasts and money. For six days the storm rages, on the seventh it is quiet, and the waters ,begin to subside. • Seven days. after .this he sends out a dove, which finds no'l'esting- place ; then a swallow, which also returns; then 'a. raven, which does 'not return. The ship rests upon a mountain, and Xisuthos offers • a sacrifice to the gods. They smell the savour, and it is good to Ahem. "The gods gathered like flies over the sacrifice. Thereupon the great goddess at her approaCh lighted up the rainbow which the god Anu had created accord- ing to his glory." Many points will be noticed in which this story is similar to the one in the 1300 k of 'Genesis,: but the point of • similarity which is most striking is that which ascribes the cause of the Deluge to the wickedness of mankind.. It is said to have been sent, as a punishment for sin. . ' *

THE DISCOVERY OF SOME ORIGINAL • FRAGMENTS.

_ . Perhaps we, had better say again that these pieces of clay are all Assyrian copies. • No original record was found with them. To find these originals in Babylon itself has been the fervent wish of ,explorers for a full generation, and the hope was entertained that even older versions might be foimd.

Those who knONNTNTIi-ai changes are wrought in stories .by the process of time and imagina- tion felt quite sure that if such earlier cOpies did come to light they would prove to be much simpler and more in harmony with the accounts of the Book of Genesis.' Two fragments came to light to confirm this belief. One of them is in the Berlin Museum, and one was purchased by the late Mr. Pier- pont Morgan. Three years ago another was found, and of this we are able to give an engraving.

In itself the bit. of hard clay is quite insigni- ficant. It is sun-dried and not baked; measures 2:1 inches At its greatest width and 2i,inches at its greatest length, and it is about of an inch thick. It had been covered with writing upon both sides and edges. That on the back is quite broken away, but. some of that on the edge can be read, and the artist who has made our drawing has shown this in-his sketch. The tablet, when entire, was, perhaps, 10 inches by 7 inches. • •

We describe this fragment in order that some idea may be formed atrie difficulties which are experienced by those Who decipher and piece Lt4gether for us these very old records.

The following is the best English 'version we can give of the lines. Unfortunately none of them are complete :

thee . . .

I will loosen

. . . . it shall sweep away all men together; • . . life before the deluge cornett; forth; . . as .many as there are, I will bring over- throw, destruction, annihilation. Build a great ship and . .

total height shall be its structure. • • • . it shall be a houseboat carrying what has been saved of life, with a strong deck cover • • . . which thou shalt make • . the beasts of the field, the birds of heaven . . . instead of a number . . . . . . and the family • • •

• This is undoubtedly part of the Deluge story, and the date of the writing is fixed by experts at 2109 B,c. The ingenuity of the reader may be exercised to complete the imperfect lines. Assuming our scholars to be correct, we have. here a story which was written before the time of Abraham, and a story, which he must have known quite well. Long centuries afterwards it came 'to the ears of Moses, who, accordin g to Jewish tradition, gathered the narratives in the form in which we have them in the Bible. Without claiming too much for, these old stories, it may be safely said that they prove that stories of the Creation, the Deluge, and the building of the Tower of Babel were common among the ancient peoples of the East, and that in their outlines they agree very closely with the records preserved in the Book of Genesis.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
SCRAPS OF ANCIENT MYTH.

There are many other. old stories written oil the, Babylonish tOlets which would interest and . amuse young 'readers if we bad. space to Copy them. Some legends refer to the duds, and it would be easy to show that the Greeks borrowed them and made them the basis of their. oWn famous myths. Thus Tu was the "'divine storm-bird," who stole thq lightning of Bel, and was punished by the fathOr of the Gods. When we read this we recognise the Creek Prome- theus Who stole the fire ; and the Greek Aphro- dite and Adonis are copies of the Babylonish Istar and Tammuz.

One curious legend tells how the seven storm- demons once warred against the moon and threatened to, devour it. SWIMS and Istar fled from the lower sky,- and the Moon-god would have been blotted out from heaven had not Bel and Ea sent Merodach in his "glistening armour" to rescue him. Those who will read this carefully will, perhaps, deteet its meaning. It is an ancient attempt to explain an eclipse of the 'Moon. • The 'Chinese tell similar 4ories. When an eclipse of the sun or moon takes place Popular belief has it that a huge dragon swal- lows the heavenly body, from whose grip it is rescued by some friendly „deity. • Just as the Greeks believed that the gods lived on Mount- Olympus, the Babylonians believed ethat they lived on Mount Bowandiz, "the moun- tain of the world," which was supposed to hold up vaults of heaven and all the stars. This belief, coming to -us from the old records, is very interesting to Bible readers, as it explains the words in Isaiah xiv. 13, 14, in Which the King ,of Babylbn is represented as saying in his heart, "I will ascend into heaven, I -will exalt my throne above the*tars of God ; I will sit also ,on the mount of the assembly of the gods in the sides of the north; I will ascend above the heights of the clouds." A VERY BIG TASK.

My dear Children,— You have truly given me a very big task to read through all your letters: I have been hard at it all week, and have not nearly finished getting through them. It looks as if I shall have to spend Sunday over the letters. We have decided to give six prizes, but who shall get the prizes will have to be kept a secret until 'next week, when the results will be de- clared. The work of selecting the winners is difficult, so I have 'phoned to Santa Claus to come and help me, and I have asked other folk to help as well, so • that we may have the best judges possible. Truly you have given all of us .a big job. Now I must not write more, as all my time is required to get through your letters, so you will forgive me if I have not written much this week.

Your loving "UNCLE JACK."

RUNCORN MANUFACTURER DEAD.

I

Mr. Duncan Mliechnie, a well-known chemical manufacturer, died on Sunday, night at the residence of his daughter, Heath House, Higher Runcorn. He was a native of Argyll- shire, and born in the year 1831. When a young man he went to Glasgow, and after a few years came to Runcorn, where he held a post in John- son's soda works. Upon leaving the latter he started the o.ld Quay Works, Runcbrn. Later he went to St. Helens, and there began a copper smelting and chemical works on his own account. He took an active part in the muni- cipal work of St. Helens; he was appointed alderman, and was mayor of St. Helens for the year 1887-1888. He was a prominent supporter of the Presbyterian Church, St. Helens.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0240.txt | included=true | included =====
IT IS REALLY NECESSARY

TO TAKE

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEM LER 13, 1913.

nutrli

WYCLIFFE PS A.

The- Rev. T. T. James gave an address to this P.S.A. on Sunday on "Lessons from the life of Dr. Livingstone." The speaker reviewed in de- tail the great work accomplished by Livingstone on behalf of the native races of Africa, a work lie began that was now steadily pursued by others. Thera were some who said this mission- ary enterprise was a failure. The People who said that looked too much upon the opening up of new countries from the commercial aspect. Christianity stood against evil, and wherever evil exists there the missionaries must go.

Mr. B. Farrar, the secretary, presided, and Mr. Yearsley took part in the service. One new member received a hearty welcome. The meeting voted a guinea towards the, ....omen and children suffering through the Dublin strike. This is the second donation from the class.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0241.txt | included=true | included =====
MEMORIAL SERVICE TO A BOY.

At St. John's Church on Sunday morning the Rev. W. Reid devoted the children's portion of the service to the memory of Sidney Smith, an old Sunday scholar and regular Sabbath morn- ing worshipper at St. John's, who recently lost his life in Northern Ontario, in attempting to save a girl companion from drowning. The sermonette was based on the story of "Gareth and Lynette," and; in closing, the preacher said that their "first thoughts on reading the news were those of gloom and pain, because a young and promising life had been snatched away pre- maturely, but clearer and more inspiring ones followed, wherein it could be seen that a bright and vigorous life was able to follow Jesus Christ so closely as to, like Him, surrender his life for another, and be found ripe and. ready for God's use in the sphere above."

===== 0002601_19131213_art0242.txt | included=true | included =====
BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN CHURCH.

The pulpit was occupied on Sunday evening by the. nev. W. A. Beckett, who spoke from the words, "Seek ye first the Kingdom of God and his righteousness." The weight and worth of the injunction rested upon the personality of the Giver of the promise which followed. The in- tention of the Saviour was to give the guidance required in the daily life and to set life's truest aim before man. The warning note was uttered against the many voices which call to distract the attention to material things. Few persons disputed the beauty of the prin- ciples of the Sermon on the Mount, but not so few doubted their practicability in the twentieth century. Their practicability, however, de- pended upon the controlling object in the life. The Kingdom of God and His righteousness flourished in proportion as the human will was brought into line with the Divine will. TO seek the Kingdom of God was to live in accord- ance with the will of God, and this was the greatest objective in life. To seek this world's happiness was to sub- ordinate first things. Without a high guiding principle life was not the success it ought to be, and the first and greatest aim of this life was to seek first God's Kingdom and His right- eousness, and then the secondary things of life would be added unto them.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0243.txt | included=true | included =====
BOLD-STREET MEN'S CLASS.

The address on Sunday afternoon was given by Mr. James Gauld,,who dealt with the ques- tion of duty to the State. The speaker selected as a basis for his remarks the word, "Render unto Ctesar the things which are CEesar's." Briefly sketching the condition of the Jews in the days of Christ, Mr. Gauld pointed out that the Jews, though a subject race, had many privileges for which to thank their Rdman con- querors. Among these were mentioned a sound currency, protection of life and property, legal equity, and religious liberty. The Pharisees were thus rightly counselled by Christ to pay their tribute as honest men in return for these benefits. As Englishmen, we, too, are indebted to our country for many privileges, which demand a return in service and devotion. As electors, as representatives on local and national governing bodies, as social reformers, and in various other ways, England claims our intellects and our energies. To deny these is to fail in our duty as Christians. Having rendered to Ccesar the things which are Ccesar's, we shall also have rendered to God the things which belong to Him. . - .

Several members joined in an interesting dis- cussion; after which the essayist replied to the points therein raised.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0244.txt | included=true | included =====
HOUSING IN ST. HELENS.

Labour Candidate's Views.

Mr. James Sexton, prospective Labour candi- date, speaking in St. Helens on Saturday, re- ferred to the housing question. They were hear- ing a good deal in St. Helens just now,, he said, about the housing problem„ and it was sug- gested that the local Council should do some- thing. The tenement houses idea had been suggested, and Liverpool had been referred to as an example. He knew something about the tenement system in Liverpool, and, if they re- ferred to the Victoria-square tenements, then he hoped their representatives would vigorously oppose the introduction of the tenement system to St. Helens. The Victoria-square tenement system was the costliest and the most cumber- some and the most dangerous system of housing the working classes that he knew. People should be spread over the land that God had given; then why in the name of health and hygiene and commonsense and physique should they put people up four or five stories high when there was plenty of land to spread them on? The price of land was the crux of the matter in St. Helens, or anywhere else. They had bought land in •Liverpool for housing purposes, and the price was so high that it could not be. used, and had to be sold again, for people to erect warehouses on. They in St. Helens had cheaper land than in Liverpool, and they should build good houses by their own Corporation. They were talking about 'giving the houses out to private contractors, and then buying them again. The past record of jerrybuilding did ' not encourage him to agree with that idea, and he hoped the people of St. Helens would insist on their porporation building and giving the people good houses to live in.

He noticed that Mr. Rigby Swift had been giving them his political viesVs about Ireland, and supporting the idea that one corner of Ulster ought to have separate treatment for itself. If that was to be so, could not they in St. Helens ask, "What price Smithy-brow and Greenbank?" In Greenbank, particularly, the population was almost exclusively Irish Nationalist, and supposing it said, "We will not have the, ruling of your Town Council. We will have a town council in Greenbank," it would just be as,feasible for them to say that as it was for M. Swift to demand that a corner of Ulster should rule the whole of ,Ireland.

The Smallpox Germ.

The germ of .sinallpox, a protozoan so in- finitesinial that it has passed through the minutest filters and escaped the trained gaze of microscopists for decades, has (says "Science Siftings ") been finally discovered by Dr. Walter Fornet, staff physician at the 'Kaiser Wilhelm Academy. Dr. Fernet claims not only to have discovered the germ, but to be able to propa- gate it. This means, we are told, that it will henceforth not be necessary to inoculate a cow or calf with smallpox virus in order to secure lymph for vaccination, and consequently that a pure culture can be secnred. The lymph won by present methods' contains numerous bac- teria, which must be exterminated bqfore it can be used for vaccination. NVhat further Dr. Fornet's discovery may mean cannot yet be told,, but it opens the way for experiments along hitherto unknown lines in the treatment of smallpox.

RAIL STRIKE DANGER.

Trouble on the Cheshire Lines Comes to a Crisis.

MEN MAY BE OUT ON SUNDAY

By "A WORKER."

Is the industrial storm which has rumbled over the local railway world about to burst upon Warrington in its full fury? . For some weeks now Warrington has been the centre of a remarkable dispute in connection with the railways worked by the Cheshire Lines Com- mittee; a group made up of three of the wealthi- est concerns in the railway world. The position, which has already been fully explained in "The Examiner," is that the platelayers and permanent way men generally have sought to improve their conditions, but have been fought at every point by the three wealthy companies. The men, who are responsible for the track which plays a more important part in the safe- carriage of human beings than anything else connected with a railway system, are paid a miserable wage. They earn on an average about 225. per week for long hours, every mipute of which surrounds them with the danger' of per- sonal injury, and even loss of life. They have to work in all weathers, without a tittle of the shelter provided in the signal box, on the engine cab, or in the guard's van, and yet, while exposed to all the elements and to grave per- sonal risk, and while absolutely essential to the working of the system, they are tke worst paid in the railway service and are worse off than many navvies, who have comparatively few risks to run.

What the Men Want. The men seek nothing unreasonable. They ask for a wage of about 245. per week, and with the great increase in the cost of living they are not asking much. Not long ago the railway directors, gentlemen who draw .k5OO and up- wards a year for a little bit of work once a month, alleged that they could not run the railways at the then existing rates. They increased fares, excursion, contract and even ordinary ticket rates were advanced wholesale, the rates for the carriage of goods were in- creased: ay, and they even charge a penny to get on their precious railway platforms! This e was done to afford a living dividend for the wealthy shareholders. What is sauce for the goose is believed to be sauce for the gander. The shareholder goose has got the bottle of .sauce. but now the platelayer gander wants Just a little drop the sauce is, stubbornly refused. _

Remarkable Patience. The platelayers on the Cheshire Lines have shown remarkable patience. When an advance was refused—an advance granted on other sys- tems—they decided not to perform special duties which for years had been heaped ,upon them, making their hours, especially in the time of fog, almost unbearable. In conse- quence of the refusal to do spectal alit, sixteen members of the union have been suspended. Several times the men have asked the Com- mittee to Meet them, so that the griqvanees might be made known, and the honest and reasonable request has been refused. The patience of the men has now reached breaking point, and unless the Committee climb down this week-end will certainly witness a strike amongst the permanent %yak men all over the Cheshire Lines.

Public Sympathy. One feature of the dispute has been the amount of public sympathy with the men. It is not so long ago sine() the directors of railway ' companies could always command the public Opinion, but since August, 1911, there has been a marked change. Since then the public have realised how they have been fleeced by the com- panies. It is a well-known fact that after the 1911 strike one company boasted of their gener- osity to their employes, whom they conceded £70,000 in the shape of is. per week increases. Immediately after there was a rise in fares, and the returns showed that out of the increase in excursion tickets alone they had received £150,000 more per annum. If they gave £70,000 to their men, where did the other £70,000 go which came Out of the public's pocket in order "to give the railway employes a living wage"? The share- holders seem to have had the best of the deal, and, knowing that, the public do not like it. The Position Serious.

' The position on the -Cheshire Lines is particu- larly serious, and, unless the Committee change their course of action there is a grave danger that the whole of the North of England will be Plunged into a great- railway strike, which would paralyse the Christmas trade and traffic A momentous meeting of the men will be held this week-end, and, should a strike occur, other companies' employes will refuse to handle goods diverted from the Cheshire Lines, and such an incident, even on a larger scale, may be wit- nessed like that which occurred in Birmingham and Liverpool in September. Surely the Com- mittee are not so superior as to be as unap- proachable as the Shah of Persia, or some other exclusive Eastern potentate. A conference would straighten matters out, and the Com- mittee should hold such a conference. They have a duty to perform to the public from whom they earn their divideads, and that is a duty to avoid a strike, at all costs. It can be avoided by giving the long harassed permanent way men a living wage.. The Postal Trouble.

Personally I hardly think, as I have said all along, there will be a postal strike this Christ- mas, but a side issue has been raised in con- nection with the dispute, which is worthy of more than passing notice. It is reported that the Territorials are to be called out to act as " blackleg " postmen and telegraphists in the case of it strike, and that by their military oath they, will be compelled to become " knobsticks." Thus, if the story is true, trade unionists will be compelled to break up the cause of trade unionism. Ido not think, however, a respon- sible Minister would ever put- such a scheme into operation, but the threat has a very pointed bearing upon the present agitation for com- pulsory military service, and it shows in the main why trade unionists are opposed to such a scheme. They know how the conscripts would be exploited to break up their own class—the workers.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0247.txt | included=true | included =====

CHILD MORTALITY.

Lancashire is the Black Spot for

Child Destruction.

===== 0002601_19131213_art0250.txt | included=true | included =====
OLD TOWNS AND NEW.

In iew .of the Conference on Children under School Age, held at the Town Hall on Tuesday evening, It may be interesting to take note of the problem of child mortality. As is well known to social students, Lancashire is the black spot in the country so far as child Mortality is concerned. There are some special reasons for this, but first of all here is a table showing exactly what the death-rates are in the towns of Lancashire, with a population of 50,000 or more. The figures deal with the years 1907 to 1910. A period of four years obviously is a fairer index of conditions than a single year, when an epidemic may create a false im- pression as to what is normal. , The first two columns of figures represent (a) the number of deaths of children between 1 and 5 years per 1,000 infants surviving the first year; (b) the number of. deaths of children within 12 months of birth per 1,000 births. • A. B. C. Name Death-rate Death-rate Birth-rate of town. 1-5, 0-1. in 1911. St. Helens 110.9 136.5 33.2 Salford 107:1 141.7 27.2 Liverpool 104.6 141.5 30.2 . Wigan 101.0 155.3 27.3 Bootle 95.7 127.4 29.9 Warrington 93.5 124.6 28.3 Burnley 93.4 171.4 23.3 Manchester 93.1 141.1 26.2 Oldham 91.1 , 137.8 23.7 Stockport 85.5 150.0 23.4 Preston 82.1 . 152.0 23.3 Blackburn 81.7 141.6 21.5 Bolton 78.6 134.0 - 22.8 Rochdale 76.4 124.4 20.7 Bury 68.9 129.9 20.6 Barrow 65.0 108.5 26.6 Blackpool 55.6 117.7 16.3 Southport 45.1 102.0 15.2

If the towns were set out in the order in which they appear for deaths under 1 year, they would follow thus: Burnley, Wigan, Preston, Stock- port, Salford, Blackburn, Liverpool, Manches- ter, Oldham; St. Helens, Bolton, Bury, Bootle, Warrington, Rochdale, Blackpool, Barrow and Southport. Sometimes the position is strikingly reversed in relation to the two columns. Of course, certain towns cannot fairly be used for comparative purposes. Seaside resorts like Blackpool and Southport are not exactly self- contained towns. They naturally attract a large residential class, and no industries are encouraged except that of catering for visitors. The average death-rate in towns having a population exceeding 50,000 in England and 'Wales is 66.1 per 1,000 for the years 1 to 5, and 117.1 per 1,000-under 1 year. The correspond- ing rates for the 18 large Lancashire towns are 85.0 and 135.4. These figures are again for the years 1907-10.

Towns Where Women Work. It is never very safe to generalise about sta- tistics, but it seems clear that the cotton towns and other towns where adult women are indus- trially employed, have a specially bad record for infantile deaths. The employment of mothers in the mills makes breast feeding a difficulty, and it is well known that the bottle- fed baby runs bigger risks than the baby fed in the natural way. The cotton towns have from 60 to 90 per...cent. of their women occupied between the "ages of 15 and 35. Warrington compares very well with other Lancashire towns for infantile deaths, and this is probably largely due to the fact that so many of its babies are breast fed. It does not stand so favourably, however, for deaths between, the ages of one and five years. This fact has been fully recog- nised by the local Health Committee, which is to be heartily congratulated upon its recent de- cision. This is, that a Lady Inspector shall devote her whole time to visit children over 12 months old until they reach school age. In due course it will be possible to discover more exactly why the death-rate is so high, but even now there are some factors which can be con- sidered. Probably unsuitable feeding has some- thing to do with it, and if so, every effort should be made to spread knowledge as to how little children ought to be fed, especially after wean- ing. •

Large Families and Small Wages. One factor ought not to be lost sight of in the South-West corner of Lancashire, viz., the Irish immigration. Naturally the influx from Ireland tends to be the greatest at the chief port— °Liverpool (with Bootle), and in the towns near- est at hand, where unskilled labourer's are in demand. St. Helens, Wigan and , Warrington get a large Proportion of Irish, as compared with the towns, say, of North-East Lancashire. A glance at the third,column showing the birth- rates for 1911 wjll indicate how far above the average are towns just mentioned. It is cer- tainly true that there is always to be found a high birth-rate among the Irish, and, as most people are aware, the risks of a high child death-rate are great where the birth-rate is high. But as most Irish mothers breast-feed their children, the risks increase after weaning. A very large proportion of the Irish population is, engaged in unskilled, and therefore badly- paid, work. The situation created is this: Large families on low wages. It is safe to say that in household economy, food receives con- sideration before housing, so that the tendency of the Wage-earners with a small income and many children is to seek the cheapest possible house. Everyone knows the disastrous effects of bad housing upon health. Epidemics soon play havoc in close quarters, and the risks of infectiorl are, of course, greater in large thah in small houses. Where, as in the case of scarlet fever, isOlatiog is promptly resorted to, these risks are much reduced. But it is quite understandable that measles should ,work 'great havoc where children are all mixed up together. In Warrington \2O per cent. of the deaths of children between 1 and 5 are due to measles.

Preventable 'Wastage.

.An old town .is more liable to a high death- rate than a new town, for many reasons. If the bulk of the house property is modern it is natural to expect that there will be some ad. vantages over dilapidated property. Modern sanitation is also more likely to be in evidence, e.g., Barrow 'is an industrial town not much smaller than Warrington. But Barrow has the advantage of a water-carriage system, whereas Warrington enjoys (if that is the right word, the more primitive pail-closet system. In this connection the experience of Leicester is wort 4 of study. The introduction•of the water- carriage system resulted in that town in an im- provement in the statistics of infantile mortality. Only Lancashire towns have been referred to, because comparisons are • best made where somewhat similar conditions prevail. It is not much use, e.g., to compare industrial towns with some of the places that are classed as towns in official publications. Hornsey, Ealing and Wallasey are rally suburbs, with picked populations, while places like Bath, Hastings, and Eastbourne are not in any sense represen- tative towns. It may be said, however, that a comparison of the big towns of Lancashire with those of Yorkshire would not be altogether flattering to the first-named county, especially in respect of the 1-5 mortality rates. Only a fringe of a big subject has here been touched, but it will be useful if any are im- pelled to search further into the facts, and to seek for remedies that shall make for life.

The present preventable wastage would grieve the heart of any intelligent stock-breeder. W. J. IL

===== 0002601_19131213_art0252.txt | included=true | included =====
Rev. H. Ogden Prefers Voluntary Effort

To Endowment Aid.

GOLBORNE-STREET SCHOOLS.

During the last few years the school of the Golborne-street Baptists has undergone an enormous change, and a greater improvement was concluded on Saturday afternoon, when the newly-decorated premises were publicly declared open by Mr. J. C. Holland, a prominent Wesleyan whose church enthusiasm is largely centred round that at Bold-street. The Pastor, the Rev. H. Ogden presided at the opening ceremony.

Without State Aid.

Mr. OGDK.N said that he ought to first explain that Mr. E. England very kindly consented to preside at that function, but something of a very urgent nature had interfered with his being .present. The speaker remarked how very grateful he was to Mr. Holland, who had con- sented to open the premises. He thought that so far as the Free Church life was concerned, Mr. Holland was one of those with whom he had been most intimate since he (Mr. Ogden) came to Warrington. He was struck that afternoon by the 'contrasts revealed that day. When he came to preach there for the first time, some five years ago, nobody ventured to suggest that within those short five years there would be such beautiful premises as those in which they were gathered. They as Baptists had always been ready, as a matter of principle, to shoulder. their own bur- dens and responsibilities for the erection of their Sunday school premises, and they had never been willing at any period of their-history to accept help from .the State, .because they, as a denomination and. as Free Churchmen, be- lieved that the State should have no voice, power or controlling agency in the work and the doings of the Free Church life. Of course, that meant that special burdens fell upon the rank and file of their church, and the money required to be raised entailed a great amount of personal sacrifice, which made them take more interest in their church than they would do if the money came to them through an endowment. He also believed that for the Sunday school they should have the very best and most arW- tic of premises, for the children, with adorn- ments, artistic arrangements and modern methods of teaching, must naturally imbibe some of the taste, and, it meant•an elevation also in the home life. They had arrived at the day when the individual churches would have to do their own religious teaching, and he viewed with great gratitude the gradual extinction of the non-provided school. Nothing would give him greater gratification than the day when they should know that their non-provided schools were absolutely abolished. (Applause.) :

===== 0002601_19131213_art0253.txt | included=true | included =====
True and Loyal Citizens.

Mr. HOLLAND said that some months ago he made a tour of inspection in company with their minister, and he was delighted to find they had such a comfortable place in which to worship. He was glad to be present to support a work which appealed to him very strongly, and with which he had been closely connected for the greater part of his life. It was a work lie could recommend to anybody who wanted to serve the Master.

If the knowledge of Sunday school work went home to the rising.generation he felt sure they would turn out true and loyal citizens. Sunday school .teaching demanded from them their best talents; he believed there was nothing more noble than to be a Sunda3P school teacher. Perhaps things would not always run smoothly in their new school, but if the teachers and young men and women would be loyal to their faith, they would grow up to be good citizens and pillars of their church. "I hope the services wilrbe well attended," said Mr. Holland, in concluding, "and that these who have attended for ninny years will remain and be loyal to it. I have much pleas- ure in declaring• the building open for worship and Sunday school teaching." (Applause.) Mr. MICHAEL proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Holland, and, in seconding, Mr. A. STILL said

HOCKEY.

STOCKTON HEATH v. STOCKPORT.

Stockton Heath were at home to Stockport on Saturday, and after a hard, keen game, were defeated by two goals to one. Last season Stockton Heath won by three goals to one. For about the .first fifteen minutes Stockport had most of the play and were continually in the home half; the home defence, however, played up strongly and held out. Play then trecame more even until about ten minutes from the interval, when Stockton Heath pressed strongly. Baxter, stopping a hard clearance from the goalkeeper, opened the scoring for Stockton Heath. Half-time: Stockton Heath, one goal; Stockport, nil. After 20 minutes, Goodwin scored for Stock- port, and just before time Mather scored a second. The defence on both sides played a -fine game throughout, both goalkeepers bring- ing off several fine saves. Chorlton are the visitors to the Cricket Ground to-day.

LATCHFORD WIN.

The visit of the Manchester Y.M.C.A. Hockey Club to Latchford did not produce a very good game. The style of game played by the Y.M.C.A. was quite different to that usually associated with Latchford. It wasn't often that the Y.M.C.A. attempted to dribble—the ball came to them anl they knocked, it' somewhere else. They are a rare bustling lot of players, and have evidently. bustled4o some purpose this season, for previous to Saturday they had only once been defeated. The score Of 4-1 in Latch- ford's favour about represents the merits of the two teams. Knight, Isherwood and Bruhl were the best of the Latchford side. The second eleven won by -7-1, their best per- formance, at Sale Cricket Ground. The visit- ors had the assistance of Minards at inside left, and he opened the scoring for them. Sale equalised,' but Minards gave Latchford a 2-1 lead for half-time. Latchford forwards and halves in the last twenty minutes came with a rush, gild five goals were actually scored by Minards and Furness; quite a surprising change.

The first eleven play their return match with Ashley at Hale this afternoon, and the team is: J. Bruhl; W. Fletcher and C. Billington ; F. W. Isherwood, J. B. Wilkinson and A. Knight; H. Fletcher, R. ANGLING.

Warrington Workingmen's

Association.

N.A. CENTRE.

Not a single member paid the Gowy a visit last week-end, and angling is practically at a standstill; Many are giving, it a rest until the Christmas holidays, when there will be a large muster at Mickle Trafford. Down South better results were obtained than for some weeks, but then these waters are out of reach of our locals. The waters near home have given such poor re- sults .as to almost exhaust: the patience of anglers. Although the season has been poor generally, some nice weights are carrying off the sets this year, with the exception of the. carial sets, and these are the worst we have ever recorded. •

Unsatisfactory Ending.

This was a most unsatisfactory ending to a 'thoroughly unsatisfactory game. • The referee .of course, was well within his right in award- ing a try, even if it had not been scored, but in similar cases other referees have ruled against a score. Aad as..Mr..Jones did not .see this incident it would have been more satisfactory had he inlet! out the claim for a try. It was a game .which did not lend itself much to criticism, :unless to say that it was of a most disapiminting character. . The Warrington for- wards were the better pack. Yet they did not work with the unison •we saw against Leeds. The Warrington half-backs were at Sixes and :leverks. PICKS OUT OF THE SCRUM.

Pithy Pars About -the N.U. Games.

It was sad .form at Salford. Warrington should never have been beaten. The try in the dark did it. This was a most questionable score. It is very doubtful if May Ever crossed the line at all. The referee, however, gave the points, And there was no chance to appeal. This gave Salford a material Lift up in the League table. They are amongst the first four. Their play does not justify it.
